ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

### **1. Overview** 

The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment is one of six independent experiments that make up the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project for which tests are conducted within the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station (ISS). 

The experiment’s Principal Investigator (PI) is Prof. James G. Quintiere of the <u>Department of Fire Protection Engineering</u> at the University of Maryland.  The experiment has two Co-Investigators (Co-Is), namely Prof. Peter B. Sunderland, of the same U. Maryland department, and Dr. John L. de Ris (FM Global, retired).  The ISS research also benefitted greatly from the work of Parham Dehghani, a doctoral student in the Fire Protection Engineering department. 

Meanwhile, Dennis P. Stocker of the NASA Glenn Research Center is the Project Scientist (PS) for ACME including BRE and the rest of the ACME investigations.  In preparatory drop testing, etc., Dr. Fumiaki Takahashi of the Case Western Reserve University (CWRU) served as the Deputy Project Scientist.  For ACME’s spaceflight operations, the NASA science team includes Drs. Uday G. Hegde and Vedha Nayagam who are also with CWRU. 

The ISS operations were conducted in two separate rounds of testing during Increments 58-59 and 63-64. More specifically, the first round of testing was conducted from Feb. to April 2019, while the secondround testing occurred from Sept. 2020 to Jan. 2021.  The first round of tests was limited to ethylene and the 25-mm BRE burner, while the second round also included tests with methane and the 50-mm burner (although not together). 

### **2. Science Overview** 

##### **A. Background** 

_This background is the Executive Summary from the experiment’s Science Requirements Document (SRD) of June 2013._ 

The goal of this research is to establish the burning conditions possible in a microgravity quiescent environment.  The approach is to do this using a burner to emulate (BRE, Burning Rate Emulator) the burning for real condensed fuels, representative of common liquids and solids.  This will be done for quasi-steady burning conditions, albeit ideal in some cases, but the BRE will range over a wide spectrum of real fuel properties.  The key properties governing steady burning are (1) the heat of gasification, (2) the surface re-radiation heat flux, and (3) the heat of combustion.  Flame radiation is also relevant in general and can be represented in terms of the laminar smoke point. Its importance in microgravity flames needs study, but the first three properties are key. 

Currently NASA assesses the flammability hazard of materials for use in space flight by an upward flame spread “pass-fail” test (Test 1), and supplements with data from the Cone Calorimeter (Test 2).  The latter test has the capability of measuring the three properties listed above.  Hence, the successful application of the BRE in microgravity can enhance the usefulness of Test 2. 

Fundamental and ad hoc testing in microgravity at quiescent conditions has shown that quasisteady flames can be supported on candle-wicks, droplets, jets and, at very low ambient velocities, small plastic samples and a burner flame.  The established flames tend to have spherical or semi- 

3 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

ellipsoidal shapes. Our preliminary work burning ethanol on planar wicks of 1 × 4 cm suggest similar shapes in aircraft and drop tower tests.  But this work showed instabilities in the drop tower, and needs further exploration.  Theoretical analysis based on microgravity jet flame length correlations suggests that a 50 mm diameter planar material could support quasi-steady combustion in microgravity for some liquid and plastic fuels.  The analysis indicates that burning depends on diameter, so several additional burner sizes will be needed to examine this effect. 

Gas burners like the BRE have been used to successfully simulate burning of a wide range of fuels in terms of their _B_ numbers and configurations over a range of Grashof numbers.  We have successfully shown that for planar burning at different angle orientations that a burner can emulate the flame standoff distribution, and even match the onset of unsteadiness to turbulent flow.  Theoretical analyses of such a problem are limited by the inability to accurately deal with flame radiation, turbulence and variable properties.  A burner simulation can be far superior as it inherently contains these effects. 

The construction of a 50 mm diameter prototype BRE burner with flame heat flux sensors has enabled us to emulate both a methanol and heptane pool fire of the 50 mm diameter, reproducing its color, flame character and shape, and heat of gasification for nearly the same surface temperatures.  We show that the BRE is capable of displaying the burning conditions in terms of the fuel properties of heat of combustion, heat of gasification, and surface re-radiant heat flux (with some likely effect of smoke point).  A preliminary 3-D plot of steady burning gives a flammability mapping in terms of these variables under conditions of gravity and atmospheric variations.  A mapping in microgravity can demonstrate the burning conditions for a wide range of real materials in terms of these properties.  In addition the effect of external radiation can indirectly be assessed.  The 50 mm burner has been redesigned, along with a 25 mm burner, to reduce edge effects, provide improved heat flux sensors with temperatures, and cooling water has been dropped from the both the burner and the heat flux sensors in microgravity experiments. 

The primary objective is to observe the burning behavior (including extinction) in long-duration microgravity for methane/nitrogen and ethylene/nitrogen mixtures on a porous circular burner of 25 and 50 mm diameter at various pressures, ambient oxygen, and CO2 (desired, to assess extinguishment).  Ignition will not be directly studied in microgravity studies, as we believe normal-gravity experiments are sufficient for its study.  Another objective is to obtain conditions that can be related to microgravity behavior of condensed fuels that embrace the scope of real liquid and solid fuels. 

We think these results can establish fuel properties that directly relate to the fire hazard in spacecraft, and these properties can be derived from NASA Test 2.  The BRE is an efficient, safe, and comprehensive technique for achieving these results. 

This version of the Science Requirements Document has been refined since the Science Concept Review (Aug 28, 2012).  The panel wrote at that time that they “strongly encourage continued development of the BRE experiment for implementation into the ACME and testing on the ISS.” We have tried to address most of their recommendations and suggestions.  In addition, work has progressed to include the development of two papers, and the commencement of drop facility testing of the BRE.  Those results have shown quasi-steady flames, and are very encouraging. 

4 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

##### **B. Rationale for Microgravity** 

_This is the ‘Justification for Extended Duration Microgravity’ section (2.5) of the experiment’s SRD from June 2013.  Equations are not included here, but they can be found in the SRD._ 

Fire in a spacecraft microgravity environment is a likely event based on limited accident data, tests to burn materials, and research that has explored simple burning scenarios.  Indeed, NASA screens the use of materials on spacecraft by its flammability Test 1. 

Currently NASA Test 1 and Test 2 are incapable of directly analyzing material fire hazards in spacecraft. There is no predictive methodology to extrapolate from these tests.  However, NASA Test 2, the Cone Calorimeter, is capable of determining heats of combustion and gasification, and surface reradiation in burning. Steady (or quasi-steady) burning rate data from the BRE in flight experiments will establish a flammability mapping in terms of these properties for microgravity. In addition, using Eq. (2), the results can establish the necessary heat flux condition for other fuels to burn in microgravity.  The BRE is an efficient way to achieve quasi-steady burning. While microgravity tests of condensed fuels have been performed and are planned, they are limited in their ability to reveal sufficient information about the diverse condensed fuels that exist aboard the ISS.  Moreover the time to achieve quasi-steady burning rate of solid fuels can be of the order of minutes as dictated by the time response of the solid.  The BRE can be turned on and off quickly, and its quasi-steady condition is only dependent on the gas phase phenomenon.  While this time is shorter, it can still be on the order of 10 seconds or more. 

The proposed tests cannot be performed in parabolic aircraft owing to considerable g-jitter disturbances.  While limited testing is possible in drop facilities, the characteristic times for the gas phase (approximately 20 s) and the burner surface temperature (approximately 40 s) preclude consideration of quasi-steady conditions.  Preliminary testing in the NASA 5.2 s drop facility indicated the approach to quasi-steady burning, but longer tests are needed to confirm this tendency.  The longer duration testing in the ISS with the efficient operation of the BRE can produce results only limited by the gas-phase response time. 

##### **C. Science Objectives** 

_The proposed objectives text presented here is the ‘Experimental Objectives’ section (2.2) of the experiment’s SRD from June 2013.  ISS testing was not carried out to address the desired second objective, where NASA effectively limited this experiment to the first and third objectives._ 

The BRE flight program seeks to establish the burning conditions in a quiescent microgravity ambient in terms of material properties.  The specific flight objectives are as follows. 

1. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25 and 50 mm diameter.  Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures.  NASAconsidered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen: 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen.  Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels. 

2. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambients of O2/CO2 (desired). 

3. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits.

##### **D. Science Success Criteria** 

_This text is from the BRE portion of the ‘Success Criteria’ section of the ACME Integrated Science Requirements Document (ISRD, ACME-SRD-001.B) from Sept. 2013, which is revision B of the version prepared for the Requirements Definition Review (RDR).  Achievement of superior success, as defined below, was effectively ruled out by NASA because its plan didn’t include the desired tests._ 

###### **6.5.1.  Minimum Success** 

- Obtain color video, burner surface temperature, and burner heat flux for two C2H4 flames that burn for at least 20 s and then extinguish. 

###### **6.5.2.  High Success** 

All of the above plus: 

- Obtain a flammability map for C2H4. 

###### **6.5.3.  Complete Success** 

All of the above plus: 

- Obtain a flammability map for CH4. 

- Measure peak temperatures (using filament and/or soot pyrometry) as a function of time for at least 5 flames. 

- Measure temperature distributions (using filament and/or soot pyrometry) at or near quasi-steady conditions for at least 5 flames. 

- Measure temperature distributions (using filament pyrometry) near the extinction limit for at least 5 flames. 

- Measure soot volume fraction profiles at (or near) quasi-steady conditions and near the extinction limit for at least 5 flames. 

###### **6.5.4.  Superior Success** 

All of the above plus: 

- Complete the desired parts of the test matrix.

### **3. Hardware Overview** 

##### **A. General Description** 

_This section is copied from section 2.A of the April 2021 version of ‘ACME Engineering Implementation’ which should be consulted for additional details.  References to other sections within the boxed text of this section refer to the ACME Engineering Implementation document._ 

_This section provides a general description a general description of the ACME engineering from the ‘3.0 Experimental’ section of the following 2014 conference paper:_ 

_Dennis P. Stocker, Fumiaki Takahashi, J. Mark Hickman, and Andrew C. Suttles, “Gaseous Non-Premixed Flame Research Planned for the International Space Station,” Spring Technical Meeting of the Central States Section of the Combustion Institute, Tulsa, OK, 16– 18 March 2014._ 

**_<u>IMPORTANT:</u>_** _The 2104 paper from which this section was extracted proceeded completion of the spaceflight hardware which was mostly launched in 2017.  As a result, it does not accurately describe the final engineering in some regards.   Therefore updates, clarifications, additional details, shortcomings, references, etc. have been included in boxed notes like this following the original description.  As a minor note, the sizes of figures were adjusted in this report, but none were cropped._ 

As previously discussed, the ACME experiments will be conducted within the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) using a chamber insert, depicted in Figure 2, which was designed to accommodate gas delivery to a variety of exchangeable burners.  Besides the chamber insert, other ACME-unique hardware includes a high-definition color camera and an avionics package, which is for data acquisition and control. 



Figure 2: Design of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) chamber insert that will be used in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on ISS. 

7 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

ACME’s chamber insert, which is ~0.4 m in diameter, provides common mounting for a variety of burners on the chamber axis (but only one at a time) and includes a retracting resistive igniter.  As depicted in Figure 3, there will initially be gas-jet burners (with inner diameters ranging from 0.4 to 3.2 mm), a small coflow burner (where the two concentric tubes have inner diameters of 2.1 and 25 mm), porous spherical burners (with diameters ranging from 6.35 to 12.7 mm), and two flat porous burners for the BRE experiment (with inner diameters of 25 and 50 mm).  Most burners will be equipped with a surface thermocouple, and each BRE burner will have two heat flux transducers.  The internal pressure of the porous burners will be measured to allow monitoring of the pressure differential across the outlet surface.  The igniter will have a crew-exchangeable tip and arm (to accommodate the various burners) and its stepper-motor drive will allow for precise positioning. 



Figure 3: ACME burner types, including from left to right, gas-jet burners, a co-flow burner, porous spherical burners, and flat porous burners for the BRE experiment. 

_Ultimately, the spherical burners were limited to a single diameter of 6.35 mm, and the fabricated gas-jet burners were limited to two sizes with nominal inner diameters of 1.3 and 2.1 mm. However, only the larger gas-jet burner was used during the spaceflight tests.  The burners are addressed in detail in section 3.C._ 

_Unfortunately, the spherical burners have not always yielded spherically symmetric flames, presumably as a result of (1) greater burner flow through the joint between the porous sphere and supply tube, and (2) heat loss to the support tube (encompassing the supply tube) causing local flame quenching._ 

_Two types of igniter tips have been used during ACME testing.  Most tests have been conducted using a loop igniter tip like those which had been successfully used in the droplet combustion experiments which preceded ACME research in the CIR.  However, some tests were conducted with a coil igniter tip, where that included the second round testing for both the Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) and Structure and Response of Spherical Diffusion Flames (s-Flame) experiments, with the exception of the latter’s first day testing.  The CFI-G investigation will also use the coil igniter tip. For more information about the igniter tips, see section 3.D._ 

The chamber insert includes three mass flow controllers (visible on the left of Fig. 2) for the delivery of fuel, oxidizer or inert (e.g., helium or carbon dioxide), and nitrogen to the burner.  The maximum fuel flow is 2 slpm (on a nitrogen basis), whereas much higher flows, e.g., 10 slpm (on a nitrogen basis), are possible for the oxidizer and nitrogen lines.  The crew can exchange the mass flow controllers to provide appropriate ranges that maximize the flow accuracy.  The gas delivery system enables on-orbit dilution of the fuel or oxidizer (but not both simultaneously), coflow, inverse flames (e.g., oxidizer delivery to the center of a coflow burner), and premixed flames, 

8 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

although the latter are not planned for any of the current ACME experiments.  Gases can also be delivered premixed to the space station where that is necessary in some circumstances, such as the blending of different fuel gases.  CIR’s standard bottle sizes are 1, 2.25, and 3.8 liters but ACME will sometimes use smaller bottles as a hazard control to limit the deficient reactant (e.g., fuel) that can be released into the combustion chamber. 

_Thus far, ACME tests were not conducted with carbon dioxide as a diluent.  Furthermore, the fuel diluted with helium was premixed prior to launch rather than on orbit.  Ultimately, oxidizer flows of up to 20 slpm (on a nitrogen basis) were possible, while the nitrogen flow was limited to a maximum of 5 slpm.  ACME has not used gas bottles smaller than 1 liter as indicated above; the bottle fill pressure was instead limited as an additional hazard control._ 

ACME’s primary flame diagnostics are a suite of imaging systems including five video cameras and two illumination systems, of which three cameras are part of the CIR facility and are already in use on orbit.  All but one of the cameras is mounted outside of the chamber.  Collectively, the imaging systems will enable color, CH*, and OH* imaging; pyrometry; measurement of the soot volume fraction; and operational support.  However, it is unlikely that all functions would be exercised at any one time as will become apparent in the subsequent discussion. 

Color imaging is provided by two cameras: a high-definition digital camera mounted outside of the chamber and a standard analog camera mounted on the chamber insert (with a turning mirror).  Both cameras are part of the ACME hardware that is now in development.  The analog camera signal will be downlinked in near real time to support the experiment operations.  Lightemitting diodes within the chamber can be used to illuminate the igniter and burner to both support the operations and provide reference images.  The 12-bit digital camera records at up to 30 full frames (1360x1024) per second, and is equipped with a motorized lens with independent control of the zoom, iris, and focus.  The digital camera will be equipped with a filter assembly with four options, where selection of a clear “filter” allows for color imaging.  Color imaging can also be carried out with a blue-green BG7 Schott glass filter, which balances the color planes to avoid saturation in the red while maintaining sensitivity in the blue and green.  The natural flame color can be approximately reconstructed, but the primary purpose of the filter is pyrometry and measurement of the soot volume fraction, again via the techniques of [23-24].  Alternately, CH* imaging can be conducted with this camera because the filter assembly includes a 430-nm bandpass filter with a 10-nm full width at half-maximum transmission (FWHM).  A 450-nm bandpass filter (again with a 10-nm FWHM) can be used with the CH* filter to effectively subtract interfering broadband soot emission.  However, the filter assembly rotates slowly so such subtraction effectively requires a separate test with each filter and of course reproducible flame conditions. 

_The video from the analog operations (i.e., ops) camera, also referred to as the internal camera, is not archived in space.  The video is instead recorded on the ground in the operations center.  Its gain was sometimes adjusted for improved imaging of dim flames, e.g., approaching extinction._ 

_The sensitivity of the color digital camera, often referenced as the ACME DataCam (i.e., Data Camera), to the dim microgravity flames was such that it was often used with long exposure times and thus low framing rates, for example on the order of 1 frame per second.  The clear “filter” is of fused silica.  Unfortunately, the thickness of the blue-green BG7 Schott glass filter and the added_ 

9 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

_antireflective (AR) coating caused too great a reduction in the red, so it does not balance the intensity of the three color planes as intended and thereby can limit the accuracy of temperature determination.  Meanwhile, rather than the 450-nm bandpass filter described above, the high definition color camera was equipped with a bandpass filter for imaging formaldehyde emissions. For more information, see the Data Camera Characterization Report (ACME-RPT-010 Rev. A)_ . 

_Imaging of the dim hydrogen flames in the Structure and Response of Spherical Diffusion Flames_ _<u>(s-Flame) experiment was particularly challenging with these, if not all, cameras.</u>_ 

Pyrometry can alternately be accomplished with CIR’s monochrome High-Bit Depth Multispectral (HiBMs) camera, equipped with a Liquid-Crystal Tunable Filter (LCTF), which allows for imaging at multiple spectra, but requires a quasi-steady flame for measurement at each desired band.  This camera has a telecentric optical system and is recorded at 512x512 and 30 frames per second (fps).  Although it has a 1024x1024 pixel array, binning has been necessary because of signal loss through the LCTF.  Both soot pyrometry and Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP) are possible, where ACME is equipped with a crew-replaceable array of 5 silicon carbide (SiC) fibers.  The fibers are orthogonal to the flame axis and the array can be translated along the flame axis to the desired position downstream of the burner, e.g., based on the downlink of the analog video.  During set up, the crew can manually orient the array so that it is orthogonal to either ACME’s color digital camera or the HiBMs camera with the LCTF.  An alternate fiber array including a platinum wire will be used for inflight characterization, which in combination with preflight calibration will enable determination of the soot volume fraction [24]. 

_In contrast to the preceding paragraph, ACME did not use a HiBMs camera for pyrometry, but instead relied on use of ACME’s color DataCam for such temperature measurements.  For an example of how this can be done, see the techniques described in the following papers: P.B. Kuhn, B. Ma, B.C. Connelly, M.D. Smooke, and M.B. Long, “Soot and Thin-filament Pyrometry_ 

_Using a Color Digital Camera,” Proceedings of the Combustion Institute, 33 (2011) 743-750. B. Ma, M. B. Long, “Absolute light calibration using S-type thermocouples,” Proceedings of the Combustion Institute, 34 (2) (2013) 3531-3539._ 

_Thus far, TFP tests have been conducted with 14-micron fibers spaced apart by 5 mm for a total span of 20 mm (in the case of 5 intact ceramic fibers), neglecting optional translation of the array. Tests have been conducted using arrays with and without the platinum wire in the furthest downstream position.  Tests have also been conducted with some of the fibers broken and subsequently removed, leaving only three fibers in the final testing for the BRE and Coflow Laminar Diffusion Flame (CLD Flame) experiments and at least the initial testing for the Cool Flames Investigation with Gases (CFI-G)._ 

CIR has a second HiBMs camera, without a filter, that will be used in combination with CIR’s illumination package for alternate measurements of the soot volume fraction.  This will be accomplished via an extinction technique previously employed in microgravity [3,6,26], where the camera and the collimated light source (with its two fiber-coupled laser diodes) are positioned at opposite windows.  The images are recorded at 1024x1024 and 30 fps.  Thus far, the primary purpose of this combined system has been the measurement of the droplet diameter for determination of the droplet burning rate. 

10 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

_A HiBMs camera without the Liquid Crystal Tunable Filter (LCTF) has been used throughout much, but not all, of ACME’s operations.  In the first round of the CLD Flame experiment, it was used without a filter for flame imaging, but this proved to be largely unsuccessful because the camera is very insensitive to blue.  Afterward, it was equipped with a red filter corresponding to the CIR illumination package for the rest of the ACME testing.  Unfortunately, that filter is sufficiently broad that significant incandescence can pass through the filter limiting its utility for determinations of soot volume fraction via light extinction, e.g., through the technique described in the following papers:_ 

- _D.L. Urban, Z.G. Yuan, P.B. Sunderland, K.C., Lin, Z. Dai, and G.M. Faeth, “Smoke-Point Properties of Nonbuoyant Round Laminar Jet Diffusion Flames,” Proceedings of the Twenty-Eighth Symposium (International) on Combustion 28 (2000) 1965-1972._ 

- _F.J. Diez, C. Aalburg, P.B. Sunderland, D.L. Urban, Z.-G. Yuan, G.M. Faeth, “Soot properties of laminar jet diffusion flames in microgravity,” Combustion and Flame, 156 (2009) 1514-1524._ 

- _P.S. Greenberg and J.C. Ku, “Soot Volume Fraction Imaging,” Applied Optics, 36 (22) (1997) 55145522._ 

_Nonetheless, soot volume fraction can also be determined from the color DataCam images._ 

_During ACME operations, the HiBMs camera is typically archived at 15 fps because of data limits for transfer and downlink operations.  And because operations are constrained by the data limits, image data from this camera are sometimes deleted without downlinking to enable additional testing.  And in some testing, HiBMs images aren’t even acquired given their lesser value._ 

_Unfortunately, the HiBMs backlight illumination is significantly obstructed by the bracket for the electrode mesh further limiting its utility for the E-FIELD Flames experiment.   This was a design trade-off necessitated primarily by ACME’s large number of cameras and other optical instruments._ 

CIR’s Low-Light-Level Ultraviolet (LLL-UV) package is a monochrome camera with an intensifier (for 280-700 nm) and a 310-nm bandpass filter (with a 10-nm FWHM) for OH* imaging.  Again as a result of the signal loss through the filter, binning is employed such that the OH* images are recorded at 512x512 and 30 fps. 

_During ACME operations, the LLL-UV camera has only been used for the Structure and Response of Spherical Diffusion Flames (s-Flame) experiment._ 

_While not discussed in the preceding section, another intensified camera was used throughout ACME testing, although its image data was sometimes deleted or not even acquired to allow the performance of additional tests.   This was the Cool Flames Investigation (CFI) camera which was generally configured with its CH* filter, where an exception is some s-Flame testing with particularly dim flames.  Due to design trade-offs, its Field of View (FOV) was partially obstructed by the arms of the Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP) fiber array when it was moved inwards to the flame.  More information about this camera can be found in its characterization report, CIR-RPT4600._ 

The ACME chamber insert will also be equipped with non-imaging optical diagnostics including both thermopile radiometers and Photomultiplier Tubes (PMTs).  There is a crew-exchangeable linear array of radiometers of which there are two versions supporting the initial set of 

11 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

experiments.  The array has a fixed position which is parallel to the chamber axis and centered (along the chamber length) relative to the chamber windows and thus most of the other instrumentation.  One array is equipped with a single wide-view radiometer with two narrowview radiometers that are downstream and two more that are upstream, where all views are orthogonal to the chamber axis, as depicted in Figure 4.  The narrow-view radiometers are intended to support studies of gas-jet flames, where ACME can accommodate shorter burners, making the “lower” detectors useful.  The other array, also seen in Fig. 4, provides for three wideview measurements of the flame, where one view is masked to nominally block radiant emission from the burner.  It was specifically designed for the BRE experiment but will be a resource available to other ACME experiments.  Given that the radiometer array can be exchanged by the crew, alternate arrays could be developed. 



Figure 4: Crew-exchangeable radiometer arrays on the ACME chamber insert: (top) standard (ACME) array of 5 radiometers, and (bottom) array with three radiometers, designed for the BRE experiment, where the lowest detector is masked to avoid view of the burner. 

12 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

_A third radiometer array was developed for the CFI-G experiment, where it is important to understand that only a single radiometer array can be used at a time._ 

_The narrow-view radiometers in the standard array are only fully sensitive in small spots, but they are each sensitive in a much greater Field of View (FOV) than represented in the preceding figure. They do not have lenses; their FOV is a result of an aperture where most of their FOV is within the penumbra which is only visible to a portion of the sensor.  Figures representing the measured FOVs of the as-built standard and BRE radiometer arrays are found in section 3.F of this document._ 

_Unfortunately, the radiometers in the BRE array typically saturated in the first round of BRE testing, so the standard array was used in its second round.  However, those radiometers similarly saturated in many of round 2 tests.  In this regard, each of ACME’s BRE and standard radiometers have a fixed gain.  Meanwhile, each sensor in the radiometer array developed for the CFI-G investigation has two fixed gains, i.e., a high gain and low gain._ 

_For more information about the standard radiometer array used for most ACME testing, see the Radiometer Characterization Report (ACME-RPT-011).  Meanwhile, you can learn about the other radiometer arrays from the BRE Radiometer Characterization Report (ACME-RPT-012) and the Cool Flames Radiometer Characterization Report (CIR-RPT-4600)._ 

An additional wide-view radiometer, that is fixed and not crew exchangeable, is positioned downstream of the burner and flame at 30<sup>o</sup> from the chamber axis.  This detector is primarily intended to support the spherical flame experiments and will have a quicker response than the more sensitive detectors that will used in the linear arrays.   Like the radiometers on the two linear arrays, it will have a spectral range of nominally 0.2-11 micron and its field of view will be established by an appropriately selected aperture. 

_The FOV of the off-set radiometer is also shown in section 3.F.  Learn more about this sensor from the Radiometer Characterization Report (ACME-RPT-011)._ 

To detect the quenching of dim flames, the chamber insert will be equipped with a set of three PMTs.  Each PMT will have a wide-angle view, a sight line that is orthogonal to the chamber axis, and will be centered (along the chamber length) relative to the chamber windows so that it is aligned with most of the other instrumentation.  The PMTs’ spectral range will be 230-700 nm, i.e., visible and near ultraviolet, but one will have a 310-nm bandpass filter and another will have a 430-nm bandpass filter (where both are with a 10-nm FWHM).  The pair of detectors will enable measurement of OH* and CH* emission, respectively.  The third PMT will be equipped with a neutral density filter to prevent saturation.  Like many other components, the PMT array can be removed from the chamber insert by the crew. 

_Unlike the radiometers, each PMT had a readily adjustable gain.  However, the PMT signals sometimes saturated when the gains were set too high.  See the PMT Characterization Report_ _<u>(ACME-RPT-013) to learn more about the three Photomultiplier Tube (PMTs).</u>_ 

The chamber insert is also equipped with six far-field thermocouples to measure the spread of the thermal field in the spherical flame experiments.  They are of limited utility for other experiments because they are positioned upstream of the burner tip.  Therefore, they will 

13 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

generally not be installed by the crew to reduce the chance of accidental damage.  There will be two versions of a four-thermocouple rake to allow a choice of near and far measurements.  Other arrays could be designed to support future experiments, but they cannot extend downstream of the burner tip without interfering with other (e.g., optical) measurements. 

_The two lone thermocouples, which are both at nominally 200 mm from the center of the spherical burner, have been installed throughout ACME testing.  The tip of one was seen to be slightly bent early in testing, as discussed and depicted in section 3.G of this report._ 

_Only the short (or far) version of the radiometer rake has been used thus far, where factors include the need for crew time to switch between rakes and the heat loss that could result from thermocouples in the long (close) rake.  The short (far) rake’s thermocouple tips should nominally be at 45, 60, 100, and 100 mm from the spherical burner center.  For thermocouples at the same distance from the burner center, the angle between the rays from the burner center to the two thermocouples should be 90 degrees.  The bead size of the thermocouples was not measured, but it is undoubtedly at least twice the size of the 0.2 mm diameter wire._ 

_The two thermocouples closest to the burner outlets on each four-thermocouple rake are type B, while the others are type K._ 

Experiment-unique hardware is also mounted on the chamber insert.  For example, to accommodate the E-FIELD Flames experiment, the insert is equipped with a high-voltage supply and a crew-removable electrode mesh which can be set between +10 and -10 kV.   The circular mesh is orthogonal to the chamber axis and is positioned (during set up) at either ~35 or ~50 mm downstream of the insulated burner.  The ion current between the burner and electrode mesh will be determined via measurement of the voltage drop across a known resistor and application of Ohm’s Law. 

_Additional images of the chamber insert can be seen in subsequent sections of this report.  The hardware specific to E-FIELD Flames is discussed further in section 2.B._ 

Additional supporting measurements will be made, where the chamber pressure is a simple example.  The ACME experiments will use the CIR’s gas chromatograph for pre- and post-test analysis of the chamber atmosphere, e.g., for oxygen, nitrogen, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, and fuel gases.  Acceleration measurements will be provided by the Space Acceleration Measurement System (SAMS) [27], which is the primary instrument for such measurements on ISS and previously served that role for the space shuttle. 

_The CIR’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentation Package (GCIP) was not used for the CLD Flame or E-FIELD Flames investigations and thus far was only sometimes used during BRE, Flame Design, and s-Flame testing.  Its use requires significant time and as such effectively reduces the number of tests that can be conducted.  However, estimates of the chamber contents were computed assuming that the fuel in a normal flame test (for example) was either fully or not at all oxidized, e.g., for unsustained ignition in the latter case._ 

_The CIR facility was equipped with a 3-axis SAMS sensor head and the acceleration measurements, along with relevant documentation, are accessible via the home page for the Principal Investigator_ 

14 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

_Microgravity Services (PIMS),_ _<u>https://gipoc.grc.nasa.gov/wp/pims/home/.  To be clear, the</u> acceleration measurements are not archived with the ACME experiment data in the Physical Sciences Informatics (PSI) website,_ _<u>www.nasa.gov/PSI.</u>_ 

All ACME experiments will be constant volume (i.e., rather than constant pressure) studies, where the chamber’s free volume will be about 90 liters.  Burner flows will generally be constrained to limit the pressure rise, and tests will typically be 0.5-2 minutes in duration. 

_The chamber’s free volume is approximately 83 liters depending on the installed hardware._ 

The ACME experiments will be configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew.  Instead, the experiments will be commanded from the ground, specifically from the Telescience Support Center (TSC) at the NASA Glenn Research Center in Cleveland, Ohio.  They can be conducted in a nominally automated mode using pre-programmed scripts.  However, it will also be possible to effectively operate the experiments in a “manual” mode, where changes can be made in response to the analog video downlink and the numerical data available in the telemetry stream.  For example, a flow rate could be adjusted to approach a stability or soot limit.  Step and ramp changes can be made in the flow rate(s) or electric field potential.  The TFP fiber array can be translated uniformly through a soot-free flame to get a continuum measurement of the temperature field.  In summary, ACME’s experiment control will allow for broad flexibility. 

_The TSC is now also referred to as the Glenn ISS Payload Operations Center (GIPOC).  Thus far, the ACME tests have always been conducted in a nominally automated mode._ 

##### **B. Hardware Shortcomings** 

In terms of hardware, BRE’s primary challenge was that the sensors for measuring the thermal radiation, which had fixed amplification, often saturated.  Although the measurements were not documented as required to meet experiment success, they were considered sufficiently critical that BRE was equipped with its own radiometer array, featuring three thermopile detectors. Estimates had been made of the signal levels based on 5-second drop tests since the hot gases tend to blanket the flame in the absence of buoyancy and thereby remain within the view of the radiometers.  As a result, the signal levels are strongly dependent on gravity.  The estimates were not part of the science requirements and may not have been sufficiently tracked in the hardware development.  Furthermore, the ISS tests lasted as long as 5 minutes, which is over four times the envisioned BRE test duration and 60 times the duration of the drop tests.  With the fixed amplification of the radiometer signals, the data was frequently lost - other than the knowledge that the limits were exceeded.  Saturation of the radiometers was partially mitigated in the round 2 testing through use of ACME’s standard array instead of the one developed specifically for BRE which was used in the first round of testing. 

The outlet surface of the 25-mm BRE burner (used in all but two of the BRE test days) became scratched and worn, as seen in Fig. 5(a), through ISS stowage and/or handling.  This degradation presumably occurred following its removal after the first round of testing as seen in Fig 5(b).  The scratches are not evident in Fig. 5(c) taken when the 25-mm burner was removed at the end of its use, but the outlet surface was clearly no longer uniformly black.  These changes are important because of their effect on the absorptivity of the burner’s copper outlet plate.  Note that Figs. 5(b) 

15 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

and (c) were rotated to nominally correspond to Fig. 5(a), i.e., with the offset heat flux transducer toward the image top and embedded thermocouples toward the bottom.

Figure 5. Changes in the appearance of the 25-mm BRE burner outlet 

16 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

As discussed in the preceding section, the ACME DataCam is unfortunately not as sensitive as had been hoped to the dim microgravity flames.  As a result, it was often used with a long exposure time limiting the framing rate, or a high gain amplifying noise in the image. 

On 3 Sept. 2020 (20247), the first day of BRE’s second round, the gas flow didn’t always transition as to the desired flow rate as intended.  The intended igniter arm was missing (but found before the next test day) and use of an alternate arm led to the concerns about ignition success and the decision to ignite at the same successful flow conditions and subsequently adjust the fuel flow rate to the desired test value.  The first attempt was successful, but the transition time was then shortened to save fuel, but it was reduced to the degree that it unfortunately prevented the intended flow transition.  Unfortunately, this shortcoming wasn’t discovered until during a review of the data days later.  As a result, the tests on that day were generally conducted at the same fuel flow rate, which was meant to be a variable.  Therefore, a make-up test day was conducted on 28 Jan. 2021 (21028) to address the science loss. 

More generally, ACME’s Mass Flow Controllers (MFCs) used to control the flow of fuel and nitrogen have unfortunately not always performed adequately, where the flow rates have not always been appropriately steady.  Steps were made to mitigate such behavior, e.g., by zero-ing the MFCs prior to each test, but they were not completely successful.  Furthermore, when an MFC is used at a small fraction of its maximum flow rate, the actual flow rate sometimes differs from that desired. 

During testing on 23 Dec. 2020 (20358), it was discovered that the lowest of the 14-micron silicon carbide fibers on the Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP) fiber array had broken.  After some discussion, testing continued that day.  Given that the translating array had five ceramic fibers, the loss of one was not a significant science loss.  But it was a concern for crew safety.  Recovery plans were developed and enacted on January 18 to remove the broken fragments from the harplike array.  Unfortunately, the next fiber was broken during the delicate operations where the fibers are only spaced 5 mm apart.  As a result, the last two BRE test days were conducted with only three rather than five fibers in the TFP array.  ACME does have alternate fiber arrays, but there were months of testing remaining and the decision was made to continue testing with only 3 fibers. 

Ignition was not always successful.  As an extreme example, it was discovered on 1 April 2019 (19091) that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed on the preceding test day, preventing further flame testing until it could be replaced.  While such circumstances are uncommon for ACME operations, BRE tests were sometimes conducted under conditions, e.g., for igniter activation and deactivation, which led to momentary flashes without sustained ignition.  Given that unplanned crew time is required to replace failed igniter tips, the BRE ops team limited - to varying degrees - the igniter voltage and the time that it was energized within the flame.  The latter was known from earlier research in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) to degrade the hot-wire igniter tip and hasten its demise.  It is notable that the flashes of flame (across the burner surface) sometimes continued briefly after the igniter was deactivated and retracted.  In one case on the first day of testing, there was excess fuel and a ‘fireball’ was ignited which promptly quenched. Ignition also sometimes failed due to a software limitation where the fuelFlowIgnitionDeltaMs parameter which specifies the relative timing of the fuel flow and igniter activation must be evenly divisible by 100 (ms).  This limitation wasn’t initially understood and the igniter sometimes didn’t energize as a result. 

17 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

While it rarely occurred, BRE tests sometimes automatically aborted (due to the controlling software script), where that is typically prior to gas flow and the activation of the igniter.  As a result, there is little lost other than the time associated with the failed test attempt.   Such aborts typically occur because of communication issues with an Image Processing and Storage Unit (IPSU). 

In addition to this section (3), shortcomings are discussed in Appendices B to E of this report and the _ACME Engineering Implementation_ document. 

### **4. Preliminary Results** 

The burner has shown that quasi-steady burning is possible for real solids with a flat surface of 25 mm diameter.  Up to now most flammability studies in microgravity have focused on flame spread with a wind for concurrent spread and at least a self-induced wind for opposed flow spread.  We already knew that this would be possible from normal-gravity research.  The burning of liquids, as in a wick of a candle flame and small droplets, has been shown to produce steady flames.  However, many droplet flames were found to extinguish owing to radiative cooling of the flame.  We see this as well in the extended ISS microgravity tests. However, this extinction was predominately seen for nominal ambient conditions of 21% oxygen and 14.7 psia.  At conditions of 27% oxygen, 10.2 psia and 34% oxygen, 8.2 psia, the flames generally persisted for 3 minutes or more.  These are all atmospheric conditions that NASA is considering for future spacecraft. 

##### **A. Changed Test Plans** 

While BRE’s proposal included desired testing with carbon-dioxide-diluted atmospheres, NASA’s plan for the ISS investigation did not include desired testing.  Therefore, the exclusion of that testing was not a change in plans. 

The most significant change was the use of ACME’s standard radiometer array rather than the BRE radiometer array in BRE’s second round of testing.  This change was made because the BRE array was found to generally saturate during the ISS tests.  The ACME array had a greater range but nonetheless still often saturated during testing. 

Significant changes were made to the ignition procedure in the early first-round testing, both relative to the preceding ACME experiments and in the first few days of BRE testing.  Compared to the other ACME burners (neglecting the outer plenum of the coflow burner), the BRE burners have a much greater internal volume.  Therefore, the fuel flow was begun before the igniter was energized, where that period ranged from a few seconds to roughly 1 minute.  The duration was typically about 15 and 30 seconds for the 25- and 50-mm BRE burners, respectively.  In contrast, the fuel flow has commonly been started when the igniter is energized in the other ACME investigations. 

Despite the duration of fuel flow prior to igniter activation, there was concern about understanding the composition of the gas initially flowing from the burner.  Therefore, on the second BRE test day (20 Feb. 2019, i.e., 19051), a no-flame ‘test’ was run before each flame test in which the burner was flushed with nitrogen at 0.15 slpm for 15 seconds.  A shortcoming of this measure was that the time between the nitrogen flushing and the start of the fuel flow was not 

18 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

precisely controlled.  Therefore, the software script used to run the BRE tests was subsequently altered to allow specification of a ‘pre-flow’ with a specified wait time between the pre-flow and the start of the gas flow for the flame.  Although the script allowed for a pre-flow of nitrogen and/or fuel, only nitrogen was used for the pre-flow during BRE’s ISS testing.  The revised script was implemented on BRE’s third test day (25 Feb. 2019, 19056) where a nitrogen flow rate of 0.15 slpm for 15 seconds continued to be used throughout the remainder of the first round. 

A wait time of 2 minutes was used in the first two 19056 tests, while a 1-minute wait was used for the remainder of testing on that day.  During the remaining round-one test days, the wait duration was consistently 50 seconds.  With the script change, the basic non-imaging data file captured the data during both the pre-flow and in the flame test, but it is important to understand that data was not acquired throughout the wait duration.  Therefore, one should pay close attention to the time stamp when reviewing BRE data prior to the start of the fuel flow because the listed time abruptly jumps. 

Although the script wasn’t significantly modified, there were changes to the ignition procedure in the second round of BRE tests.  It was concluded that the nitrogen pre-flow wasn’t needed for the first test of the day or the first test after the chamber was evacuated and refilled.  The wait time between the pre-flow and the start of the fuel flow was also increased to 5 minutes throughout the second round, whereas it was 50 seconds in most of the first-round tests.  This was done to ensure that the atmosphere in the chamber was quiescent prior to the start of the fuel flow.  Preflow durations and flow rates were also somewhat different.  In the tests on the first round-two test day (20247), which featured the 50-mm burner, the pre-flow was at ~0.5 slpm for 5 or 10 seconds.  On the two subsequent test days (20261 and 20279), the pre-flow was at 0.19 slpm for 3.2 seconds.  For the remaining test days (20339, 20346, 20352, 20358, 21020, and 21028), the pre-flow was instead 0.16 slpm for 5 seconds, where that includes the final test day which also featured the 50-mm burner.  As such, the pre-flow differed significantly between the two test days with the larger BRE burner. 

A few minor changes were made to the script after the addition of the pre-flow to flush the burner’s internal volume: 

~2020-03-17 flow ramp hold was fixed so that the hold values were achieved exactly ~2020-12-18 ignition detection was disabled prior to igniter arm retraction so that the retraction wasn’t interrupted 

The first change was implemented between the first and second rounds of testing, while the second was made during the second round.  The final version of the script will be included in the upcoming _ACME/BRE Experimental Procedure_ document. 

While a minor factor, BRE made use of the coiled type of igniter tip in second-round testing as compared to the standard loop-type igniter used in the first round.  BRE ignition seemed to require significant ignition energy and the coiled igniter design is believed to be more robust.  The coiled igniter tip was developed as an alternate after ignition challenges with the Coflow Laminar Diffusion Flame (CLD Flame) experiment in late 2017.  The development of the coiled igniter tips continued even though it was later determined that the igniter had not been properly aligned with the burner outlet in the early CLD Flame testing.  The two igniter designs are discussed and depicted in the _ACME Engineering Implementation_ document. 

In contrast to BRE’s success criteria (discussed in section 2.D), which identified the importance of 

19 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

measuring the flame temperature, BRE’s first round testing seldom made use of Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP).  In contrast, TFP data was acquired in a significant fraction of the second-round testing.  In this regard, TFP use was important because the BRE flames quickly became non-sooty even with oxygen concentrations of up to 40% (by volume) so measurements couldn’t be made via soot pyrometry. 

Tests were conducted as planned at atmospheres being considered for future spacecraft, featuring elevated oxygen concentrations and reduced pressures (nominally maintaining the oxygen partial pressure), but more of the testing was conducted at roughly atmospheric pressure with elevated oxygen concentrations.  The latter tests were carried out in a broader exploration of material flammability in microgravity. 

##### **B. Overview of Experiment Test Operations** 

_While not yet prepared, additional details will be made available in the ACME/BRE Experimental Procedure document._ 

_The narrative description of the experimental procedure is an excerpt from the ‘Methods / Experimental’ section of the following conference paper._ 

_Y.-C. Chien, J. Tinajero, D. Stocker, U. Hegde, and D. Dunn-Rankin, “Electric Field Effects on Flames in Microgravity on the International Space Station,” 2018 Spring Technical Meeting of the Central States Section of The Combustion Institute, Minneapolis, MN, 20-22 May 2018._ 

_While the paper was written for the Electric-Field Effects on Laminar Diffusion Flames (E-FIELD Flames) experiment, much of the discussion applies to ACME in general, including the BRE investigation.  The figure referenced in this excerpt is not included herein.  Clarifications and additional information, not in the conference paper, are included in boxed notes like this._ 

The CIR experiments are configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew.  Thus far, ACME’s activity has included the majority of the crew members, regardless of their nationalities.  Most often the astronauts and cosmonauts exchange gas bottles, mass flow controllers, and igniter tips.  In the latter case, the igniter tip exchange can occur after failure or proactively before failure when the crew already has changes to make changes in the interior of the insert.  While these activities may seem mundane, the crew play a critical role in setting up the hardware for each experiment.  As an extreme example, there were crew activities on 8 separate days in Sept.-Oct. 2017 to configure the CIR and ACME hardware for the first ACME experiment, namely the Coflow Laminar Diffusion Flame (CLD Flame) experiment.  In contrast, only two crew days were required to reconfigure the hardware for the first E-FIELD Flames tests because of the initial use of the same coflow burner. Planning is critical because crew activity is scheduled three weeks in advance, for which detailed procedures must be prepared and uplinked to the ISS. 

_BRE has its own unique burners and it was the first ACME experiment conducted with large flames, as compared to those in the CLD Flame and E-FIELD Flames investigations.  That required changes to some optical elements for BRE as well as subsequent ACME experiments.  BRE was also the first experiment to use the CIR facility’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentation Package (GCIP).  As a result, the initial BRE set-up included 9 days of crew activity, mostly by Canadian astronaut David Saint-Jacques, although that includes 4 days in which hardware was either gathered or stowed._ 

20 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

All CIR tests are remotely commanded from the ground from NASA’s Glenn ISS Payload Operations Center (GIPOC) in Cleveland, Ohio.  Typically, four to six people staff four consoles during the testing.  The CIR hardware is controlled from the Rack Officer (RO) and Data Management Officer (DMO) consoles, and the ACME hardware is controlled from the Payload Developer (PD) console. Two shifts of personnel are scheduled to staff these three consoles on test days because they typically last 14 hours with the many pre- and post-testing activities.  Given that the tests are remotely commanded, the operations are carried out in the day and evening in Cleveland, e.g., from 7 AM to 9 PM.  A day’s tests, i.e., with flames, are normally conducted in a period of 10 hours of less.  Tests are normally conducted on three weekdays per two weeks.  On intervening weekdays, the image data is downlinked and the crew often swap one or both gas bottles. Transferring and downlinking data from a day’s tests also requires a 2-shift day for the RO and DMO consoles, where it is always necessary to verify that data was downlinked without errors and that data is not missing.  Those two positions are also staffed to guide and oversee the crew activity.  As a result, the CIR consoles are normally actively staffed on four weekdays per week.  A team of four carry out the detailed planning for the CIR and ACME console staff, and the daily operational plans typically exceed 50 pages on days including testing. 

The science console allows for review of the analog video and non-imaging telemetry (at 1 Hz) in near real time but doesn’t allow for commanding.  In the case of E-FIELD Flames, arrangements were made so that the analog video is streamed to a secure computer at UC Irvine to allow the research team to participate in test operations and to recommend adjustments to the day’s planned parameters.  That team does not see the telemetry during the testing, but is in communication via phone and e-mail with their NASA research partners at the GRC ISS Payload Operations Center (GIPOC).  While there are many preparatory steps, the tests are ultimately conducted using an uploaded software script and parameter files.  In advance of the test day, the UC Irvine science team plans the nearly 60 parameters in the voltage sweep script and specifies other parameters that are configured outside of the script, such as camera settings.  It is also possible to effectively conduct tests in a ‘manual’ mode, where parameters are changed in response to the analog video downlink or telemetry.  For example, the electric field potential can be adjusted to approach a stability or soot limit. 

_Provisions have been made to enable the investigators from all ACME experiments to remotely view the analog video while they communicated verbally with NASA science personnel in the operations center.  Thus far, all ACME flame tests, including those for BRE, have been conducted in an automated manner using software scripts and extensive parameter files._ 

Typically, ten E-FIELD Flames tests are conducted per test day. Despite the short test durations, the low rate results from several unavoidable factors.  After a test is conducted, the 125-Hz nonimaging data is downlinked for immediate review by the science team.  A small sample of images are often downlinked to ensure that the camera exposures are appropriate, where the times for those images are typically selected based on non-imaging data to provide extreme examples.  The review helps to verify parameters or plan for adjustments in subsequent tests, e.g., to select the voltage range after determining where the ion current saturates for the given flame conditions. After a test is conducted, a fan circulates the chamber atmosphere after which the chamber is vented (to space) back to atmospheric pressure, if needed.  Midway through a day’s E-FIELD Flames tests, the atmosphere is circulated for 15 minutes through a filter cartridge filled with molecular sieves to capture any soot and adsorb at least a portion of the combustion products, e.g., carbon dioxide, water vapor, and incomplete combustion products.  The tests are only 

21 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

conducted when both Ku and S band communication is available through NASA’s Tracking and Data Relay Satellite (TDRS) System.  Depending on the various orbits, there are periodic communication outages with a loss of signal (LOS) that can be a short handover (from one satellite to another) or can last as long as 30 minutes. 

_About eight flames were ignited per test day in BRE’s first round, neglecting the day in which no tests were conducted because the igniter had unknowingly failed at the end of the preceding test day.  The nominal durations were typically planned for 2 or 3 minutes but some tests were 5 minutes long.  Meanwhile the second round of BRE had roughly 15 tests conducted per day, but the durations were nominally 2 minutes at most._ 

_Unlike E-FIELD Flames, the BRE experiment also made use of the CIR’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentational Package (GCIP) to measure species concentrations of the atmosphere in the combustion chamber.  There is no sampling probe and measurements are only made before, between, or after tests when the chamber atmosphere was ‘stirred’ through use of a fan.  The GC measurements were made on roughly 60% of the test days with flames in the first round, specifically on 19039, 19063, 19066, 19070, 19087, and 19094.  In the second round, such data was acquired on less than half of the test days, specifically on 20247, 20261, 20279, and 20352._ 

_Like E-FIELD Flames, BRE made periodic use of the adsorber cartridge, for example between atmospheres and after GC measurements, if any, were made._ 

A variety of constraints limit the number of tests that can be performed, both overall and on a daily basis.  The next combustion experiments in the CIR will feature solid fuels and focus on flame spread and material flammability.  That follow-on plan limits the duration of ACME operations, where each ACME experiment is allotted four months for testing.  Although it requires additional crew time, these allotments are split into two rounds so that the investigators have a break from testing for careful review of the early results so that they can maximize the scientific outcome of their second round.  This paper reports on early results from the first round of E-FIELD Flames. 

While nitrogen is available from the space station, the combustion testing is limited by the available gas bottles that were and are launched to support the tests.  For E-FIELD Flames and most of ACME’s experiments, the test matrices used to plan those gas resources were established in 2010.  At that time, NASA limited the scope of the ACME tests matrices with the intent of focused experiments with limited objectives rather than comprehensive experiments.  As such, the general target was 50 tests per experiment.  For most ACME experiments, the limiting gas constraint is the oxygen/nitrogen bottles used to fill the chamber.  This resource constraint is exacerbated by the CIR because it is equipped with a pair of redundant safety timers to limit the fuel that can flow into the combustion chamber (where the timers can be set for the oxidizer flow in the case of inverse flame testing).  The timers can only be reset by venting the combustion chamber.  Therefore, the timer limit combined with other constraints effectively corresponds to the day’s maximum flow time for the fuel (or more generally, the limiting reactant).  For E-FIELD Flames, the safety timers are set to the maximum value of 999 seconds, which corresponds to the 10 tests typically completed per day. 

_The 999-second limit on the valve safety timers has thus far been common for all ACME testing. However, the timers can be reset to 999 by nearly completely venting the chamber.  Sufficient time_ 

22 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

_and gas resources are needed to vent and refill the chamber and still conduct tests afterward, but this was done on most BRE test days.  The only exceptions were 19039, 19063, and 19091 where in the last case no flames were ignited because the igniter had unknowingly failed at the end of the prior test day._ 

Another constraint is the available time in a day, where the console personnel and ISS resources, such as the video downlink, are planned for a limited number of hours.  When necessary for the research, NASA does have continuous 24-hour/day operations for some non-CIR experiments. But as discussed, the ACME tests are typically two minutes or less in duration, so the testing is almost always limited to the planned durations. 

Data limits are yet another constraint, where the on-orbit hardware can store a limited amount of data.  More importantly, 127,000 MB is the most that can be reasonably managed in a twoshift transfer and downlink day.  Therefore, tests days are limited to this amount of data, where image data from select cameras is often archived at reduced rates to mitigate this challenge. Nonetheless, the limit sometimes forces decisions to abandon recorded images. 

This detailed description of operations and hardware provides the context under which the ACME experiments are conducted.  …

Note that it is comprehensive and includes the earlier testing conducted in the preceding increments.  A minor correction was made where 19094 is now identified as April 4.  It accidentally been identified as April 5 in the 30-day reports, although the testing did extend past midnight in Cleveland, OH so the final tests were conducted on April 5. Nonetheless, the BRE (and more generally ACME) test days and numbers are identified by the date on which the test day began, even if they occurred after midnight (whether on the ISS or in Cleveland)._

##### **C. Summary of Preliminary Results** 

<mark>Figure 6 shows a typical test of ignition and flame growth.  Although extinction occurred at 45 s with the flame still growing, the heat flux was nearly steady by 30 s.  Therefore, a heat of gasification (</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>) could be estimated.  This was done by using the arithmetic average of the center and offset heat flux sensor as the net average heat flux.</mark> 



**Figure 6.** Flame growth from ignition to self-extinction in test 19056F1.  The conditions are ethylene/N2,  _hc_ = 24 kJ/g, 4.55 g/m<sup>2</sup> s, 27% O2, 10.2 psia, _L_ = 0.66 kJ/g, extinction at 45 s. 

<mark>Figures 7 through 10 illustrate results for nominal atmospheric conditions of 26.5% oxygen and 0.7 atm (10.2 psia), and 34% oxygen and 0.56 atm (8.2 psia), respectively with two figures each. Both use 50% mixtures of nitrogen and ethylene giving a fuel mixture heat of combustion of 24 kJ/g.  Both tests reached quasi-steady conditions and were terminated by shutting off the fuel. The plots of transient heat flux shown by Figures 8 and 10, show the condensation effect removed after 80 s and 40 s respectively and arithmetic average of the two heat flux gauge measurements at the end of each test is recorded as steady surface heat flux which are used to obtain heats of gasification.</mark>

This is a profound result indicating a common plastic could steadily burn under these NASA proposed atmospheric conditions.</mark>

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>The experimental flammability maps are shown in Figures 14 and 15.  Both plots indicate a decreasing, inverse asymptotic-like curve that approaches zero, for heat of gasification with mass flux.  It is surprising that the data cluster about a nearly singular trend.  However, both plots show the heat of gasification is up to 20% higher for the lower heat of combustion, 23.6 kJ/g.  Our experiments in the University of Maryland lab have shown, with the BRE in 1-g, that similar results for propane-nitrogen mixtures have the opposite behavior with heats of gasification decreasing with heat of combustion for the same mass flux.  But again, the data mostly cluster about one trend line except for very low heats of combustion.  However, the 1-g corresponding heat of combustion data shift far to the right and down from the microgravity data of Figures 14 and 15. For example, the 1-g data would indicate ( 𝑚</mark> ̇ <mark>”, 𝐿 ) points of about 30 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, 2 kJ/g and 10, 0.5. Moreover, our previous work in a 5- s drop facility suggested similar data trends but with an apparent vertical asymptote at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, rather than 1 as shown here.  Obviously, the current data at durations of minutes are more accurate than those limited to 5 s.  However, it has been shown that the ISS heat flux data even at self-extinction are theoretically nearly steady. Figures 14 and 15 include the heats of gasification derived from the self-extinguished endpoint data as well.  Remarkably, the self-extinguished not-so-steady data follow the same trend as the steady data.</mark> 

<mark>Let us interpret Figures 14 and 15 in terms of the flammability of real materials in microgravity. Typical melting plastics have heats of gasification ranging from about 2 to 3 kJ/g, with effective heats of gasification for charring plastics above 4 kJ/g.  Some selected literature values for materials with heats of combustion in the BRE range are listed in Table 2.  The accuracy of the calorimeter heat flux is found to be less than the two sensors, thus, we relied on the arithmetic average of the two heat flux sensors data for obtaining the flammability map.  However, same results are shown considering the calorimeter data.  Figure 14 would suggest that polyethylene (PE) and polypropylene (PP) would burn in microgravity at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.  Nylon and PMMA would burn at about 2.5 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, and the phenolic glass fiber composite would burn at about 1 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.</mark> 

**Table 2.** Materials close to BRE heats of combustion and 

corresponding heats of gasification 

|**Material**|**_LHV_, kJ/g**|**_L_, kJ/g**|
|---|---|---|
|PE|43.4|1.9|
|PP|44|2.0|
|Nylon|28.8|1.5,2.4|
|PMMA|24.2|1.6,2.3|
|Phenolicglass fibers|22.0|7.3|



<mark>BRE tests in orbit were finished with 196 successful ignitions. The measured heat flux and the end point data of the long duration tests (i.e., the tests that fuel was terminated as opposed to the tests that self-extinguished) were used to obtain heats of gasification.  The measured heat flux for all quasi steady tests are shown in Fig. 14.  The heats of gasification of these data from all the tests vs. fuel mixture mass flux are shown in Fig. 15.  One important fire safety consideration, based on the findings of this study, is that solid materials with heats of combustion in the range 23.6 to 47.2 kJ/g and heats of gasification from about 1 to 10 kJ/g can burn in microgravity.  The NASA alternative flammability Test 2 [1] using the Cone Calorimeter can measure these material properties. As this study develops a more complete understanding, NASA may wish to put more emphasis into Test 2.</mark>

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>Regression analysis was performed on the experimental data recorded from the quasi-steady tests and the burning rate vs. parameter  is shown in Fig. 16.  The symbol  indicates the importance of each design parameter.  As shown, the oxygen mole fraction, radiant fraction, and heat of gasification are the factors with largest exponents.  The larger the exponent, the greater its effect on the burning rate.</mark> 

<mark>Moreover, the flammability map based on our theoretical analysis is shown in Figure 17.  It shows similar asymptotic trend and clearly indicates lower pressure and higher oxygen concentration as favorable conditions for long duration burning.</mark>

<mark>The imaging data from ops camera and ACME camera are used to create videos and image sequences for the tests.  Many of the first tests with the 50-mm BRE burner were not at the intended flow rate.  A make-up day was carried out to accomplish that day’s scientific objectives. The plan for round 2’s second day contained a combination of pure and diluted ethylene at 3 different ambient conditions.  Pure methane was the fuel used for testing on the second round’s third test day at 3 different nominal ambient conditions.  It was observed that methane was hard to ignite compared to ethylene and flames tend to self-extinguish faster even at a high oxygen concentration.</mark>

2. P.H. Irace, H.J. Lee, K. Waddell, L. Tan, D.P. Stocker, P.B. Sunderland, and R.L. Axelbaum, “First Observations of Long Duration Microgravity Spherical Diffusion Flames Aboard the International Space Station,” _Combustion and Flame,_ 229 (2021) 111373. <u>https://doi.org/10.1016/j.combustflame.2021.02.019</u> 

3. Venzon, R., 2021, “Thin-Filament Pyrometry and Analysis of Extinction Onset Temperature for Ethylene Flames on the Burning Emulator in the International Space Station,” University of Edinburgh. 

4. A. Wright, P. Dehghani, P. Sunderland, and J. de Ris, “Using A Burning Rate Emulator to Analyze Flame Extinction Time on the International Space Station,” poster, 12th U.S. National Combustion Meeting, virtual, 24-26 May 2021. 

##### **D. Surprises and New Discoveries** 

Initial surprises and discoveries include: 

- (1) It is found out that quasi-steady burning on a flat surface, representing a pool fire, can exist in microgravity. Ambient oxygen mole fraction plays the most significant role in this regard. The quasi-steady flames were observed for nominal ambient oxygen mole fractions of 0.265 and above. 

- (2) Although ambient pressure has a weak effect on the quality of burning, it affects radiative heat loss by changing the partial pressures of radiating species, i.e., H2O and CO2. 

- (3) Methane nitrogen-generated flames self-extinguished within a minute for all ambient conditions.  That indicates that the burn time depends on the fuel chemistry. 

- (4) Ethylene flames, which tend to soot under normal gravity conditions, did not soot under microgravity conditions. Sooting only occurred upon ignition and disappeared quickly after ignition.  Nitrogen dilution reduced the amount of soot. 

- (5) Radiative loss fraction for the flames in microgravity can reach about 60% compared to 30% for the normal gravity flames. 

- (6) TFP measurements of four flames confirmed that critical temperature range for extinction is 1100 to 1200 K.  This is confirmed by numerical simulations. 

- (7) A It was shown that the BRE can emulate several liquids and solids including Acetone, Ethanol, Methanol, PMMA, Nylon, Phenolic glass fiber, etc. as demonstrated by a flammability diagram, as a plot of heats of gasification vs. the mass burning rate, based on the steady average surface heat flux. 

- (8) For the first time, two limits of extinction were obtained on a plot of heat of combustion vs. heats of gasification.  The plot shows the flammability region for each nominal ambient oxygen mole fraction with radiative extinction for large flames and another extinction limit for small flames. 

##### **E. Accomplishment of Approved Objectives** 

Two of the three approved objectives were accomplished through the ISS experiment, where NASA did not intend to address the desired second objective through the investigation. 

39 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

The objectives are presented here with brief summaries of the findings; readers are encouraged to refer to section 4.C for more information. 

_<mark>1. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25- and 50-mm diameter. Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures. NASA-considered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen. Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels.</mark>_ 

<mark>An elevated oxygen concentration in the ambient enhanced flammability and quasisteady flames for nominal oxygen mole fractions of 0.265 and above. Pressure had a minimal effect on the quality of burning.  The microgravity flames corresponded to the combustion of a range of liquid and solid fuels.</mark> 

_<mark>2. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambient of O2/CO2 (desired).</mark>_ 

<mark>As planned, this objective was not investigated in the conducted tests.  It would be a good candidate for further exploration in a follow-on experiment.</mark> 

_<mark>3. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits.</mark>_ 

<mark>All flames ignited in normal atmosphere self-extinguished within 40 s.  In contrast, oxygen mole fractions of 0.265 and above resulted in quasi-steady flames.  Large flames extinguished due to radiative heat loss, while small flames quenched at a second limit.</mark> 

##### **F. Additional Objectives Achieved** 

An empirical correlation for the radiative loss fraction was obtained through a dimensional analysis of the radiation data.  The correlation was used to predict the flames’ radiative loss fractions from their mass burning rate, oxygen mole fraction in the ambient, chamber pressure, and heat of combustion.  This result was used to correct the heat of combustion in the analytical solution for these flames. 

##### **G. Impacts, Benefits, and Products** 

BRE yielded important data relevant to spacecraft fire safety.  Quasi-steady burning corresponding to various condensed-phase materials (such as PMMA and Nylon) was observed in microgravity despite the absence of air flow, where the cessation of ventilation is an established step in putting out spacecraft fires.  The ongoing combustion without air flow was notably seen in elevated-oxygen atmospheres which are being considered for use in future spacecraft, whereas such flames in a normal atmosphere of 21% oxygen self-extinguished in a short time. 

While BRE’s results are still preliminary, they suggest that NASA may want to increase use of its alternative flammability Test 2, with the cone calorimeter, where that test can measure material properties corresponding to the microgravity flames observed with the BRE burner. 

##### **H. Patents** 

Thus far, no patents have been filed from the BRE research. 

40 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

### **5. Follow-On Experiments** 

A study of the burning behavior in an ambient of O2/CO2 (the desired objective) is a good candidate for a follow-on experiment because of the utility of CO2 in spacecraft fire extinguishers.  We would also like to expand the testing to include propane and propylene to emulate additional condensed fuels. Furthermore, the addition of external radiation to the experimental apparatus would improve the simulation of real fires.

**Appendix B. 30-Day Operational Accomplishments Report for ISS Increments 57 and 58** 

31 May 2019 

**30-day Operational Accomplishments Report … for the International Space Station (ISS) Increments 57 and 58** 

**ACME Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments BRE Burning Rate Emulator** 

_BRE is one of several independent experiments that are part of the ACME project, which uses a single modular set of hardware to carry out studies of flames burning gaseous fuels in the ISS’ Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR).  The investigation will be carried out in two separate rounds, where the first round began in Increment 58, so there is nothing to be reported for Increment 57._ 

Prepared by the PI team and Dennis Stocker (dennis.p.stocker@nasa.gov), the ACME Project Scientist, who is the primary contact for BRE and ACME information.
