#### **I. Introduction** 

The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment is conducted in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station, as part of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project.  BRE is focused on spacecraft fire prevention.  More specifically, BRE’s goals are to improve our fundamental understanding of materials flammability, such as extinction behavior and the conditions needed for sustained combustion, and to assess the relevance of existing flammability test methods for low and partial-gravity environments.  A flat porous burner fed with gaseous fuel simulates the burning of solid and liquid fuels, where measurements are made of the thermal feedback (i.e., to the burner) upon which the vaporization of condensed-phase fuels is dependent.  A small number of gaseous fuels (including mixtures with inert gases) are used to simulate the burning of fuels such as paper, plastic, and alcohol by matching properties including the heats of combustion and gasification, the surface temperature, and smoke point.   The experiment’s results can establish fuel properties that 

45 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

directly relate to fire hazards in spacecraft, and if effective in microgravity could provide an efficient, safe, and comprehensive technique for assessing flammability and selecting fireresistant spacecraft materials. 

BRE’s specific experimental objectives are as follows, where it must be emphasized that NASA does not currently plan to carry out the desired tests for objective B. 

- A. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25 and 50 mm diameter. Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures. NASA-considered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen. Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels. 

- B. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambients of O2/CO2 (desired). 

- C. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits. 

The observations will enable quantification of the microgravity behavior of condensed fuels (i.e., liquids and non-charring plastics). 

Microgravity studies of flames allow for a range of simplifications and provide various advantages over normal-gravity studies.  For example, the flicker of flame, which is a buoyancy-driven (i.e., gravity-driven) instability, is eliminated yielding quasi-steady flames in microgravity.  Length scales are increased in microgravity flames facilitating analysis of the flame structure. Momentum-dominated flames, which are important for most practical combustion, can be studied at low velocities to simplify analysis.  Microgravity flames tend to have a much stronger sensitivity to their atmosphere and exhibit a much broader range of characteristics than normalgravity flames because of the near absence of buoyant entrainment.  The long residence times in microgravity flames can lead to strong soot production, but many microgravity flames are soot free.  The absence of buoyancy makes microgravity flames advantageous for studies of limit and stability behavior where chemical kinetics are important.  With these advantages, microgravity testing can lead to new understanding of the complex nature of combustion – regardless of the gravitational environment of the combustion application. 

#### **II. Methods/Research Operations** 

##### **A. Discussion of Method/Protocol** 

BRE and other ACME experiments are configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew. The crew’s role is to set up the hardware, gas bottles, etc. for each set of tests.  The most common changes include the gas bottles on the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) and the mass flow controllers on the modular ACME chamber insert. 

All testing is commanded from the ground, specifically from the Glenn ISS Payload Operations Center (GIPOC).  The science portion of the testing is typically conducted in a nominally automated mode using pre-programmed scripts.  However, it may alternately be conducted in a ‘manual’ mode, where changes are made to a control parameter in 

46 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

response to the analog video downlink and the numerical data available in the telemetry stream.  For example, gas flow rate(s) can be adjusted after quasi-steady data has been collected.  But testing has not been conducted in a ‘manual’ mode thus far for BRE or ACME in general. 

Testing is not conducted when thrusters fire or spacecraft dock or undock from ISS to ensure an acceptable acceleration environment. 

Data is downlinked between each set of tests, i.e., day of testing, to allow for preliminary analysis and requisite planning for subsequent testing. 

#### **III. Results** 

##### **A. Baselined Activities Completed – Pre-flight, In-flight, Post-Flight** 

The first round of BRE tests began in February 2019, where a total of 45 flames were ignited during Increment 58.  That round was completed in April 2019, where the latter portion will be covered in the upcoming Increment 59-60 report.  Meanwhile, the second round of BRE testing is expected to begin near the end of Increment 60 and continue into Increment 61.  The ‘interruption’ in the BRE testing was intentionally planned years in advance to enable the PI team to review the initial results in detail and with that knowledge plan the second round of tests to maximize its scientific outcome. 

All of the first round of tests were conducted with the 25-mm diameter BRE burner while burning ethylene (C2H4) - sometimes diluted with nitrogen.  The tests were carried out at a range of chamber pressures and oxygen concentrations, generally representative of possible crew environments in future spacecraft or habitats.  More specifically, testing was especially focused on atmospheric conditions at (1) 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; (2) 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and (3) 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen.  In all cases, the atmosphere was diluted with nitrogen and any incomplete combustion products (e.g., water vapor and carbon dioxide) from preceding tests. 

BRE does not have living subjects like a life science experiment, but the tests are independently conducted with two different gaseous fuels, methane and ethylene.  Both are simple hydrocarbon gases for which the chemistry is well understood.  Methane (CH4) is the primary component of natural gas and tends to produce low levels of soot given the absence of carbon-carbon bonds.  Meanwhile, ethylene (C2H4), is highly sooting because of its carbon double bond.  Tests are conducted with pure fuel and fuel mixed with nitrogen, where the fuel and nitrogen was premixed on the ground prior to launch for some cases.  But the ACME hardware also allows for simultaneous flow of fuel and nitrogen to the burner, allowing for on-orbit fuel dilution and testing at a continuum of fuel concentrations. 

47 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

There are no samples (e.g., of soot) collected for BRE, where all of the ISS results are electronically transmitted to the ground.  That is furthermore the case for all currently planned ACME experiments. 

A summary log of the flames ignited for BRE during Increment 58, including a limited overview of the test conditions, is found in Table 1.

##### **B. List of Preflight, In-flight, Postflight Anomalies** 

**1. Mission anomalies** 

There were no significant anomalies hindering BRE operations during Increment 58. 

###### **2. Any other difficulties in obtaining scientific data** 

BRE’s primary problem was that the sensors for measuring the thermal radiation often saturated.  Although the measurements were not required to meet experiment success, they were considered sufficiently critical that BRE was equipped with its own radiometer array, featuring three thermopile detectors.  Estimates had been made of the signal levels based on 5-second drop tests since the hot gases tend to blanket the flame in the absence of buoyancy and thereby remain within the view of the radiometers.  As a result, the signal levels are strongly dependent on gravity.  The estimates were not part of the science requirements and may not have been sufficiently tracked in the hardware development.  Furthermore, the ISS tests lasted as long as 5 minutes, which is over four times the envisioned BRE test duration and 60 

48 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

times the duration of the drop tests.  With the fixed amplification of the radiometer signals, the data was frequently lost - other than the knowledge that the limits were exceeded.  An engineering review failed to identify an acceptable solution and BRE testing continued unchanged. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

The radiometry array proved problematic as its sensors were mostly out of range and quickly became saturated.  However, for some of the small size flames, these data were complete.  Those data will be vital to assessing the role of flame and combustion product radiation in the destiny of the flames. 

Ignition was not always successful, where BRE tests were sometimes conducted under conditions, e.g., for igniter activation and deactivation, which led to momentary flashes without sustained ignition.  Given that unplanned crew time is required to replace failed igniter tips, the BRE ops team limited - to varying degrees - the igniter voltage and the time that it was energized within the flame.  The latter was known from earlier research in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) to degrade the hot-wire igniter tip and hasten its demise.  It is notable that the flashes of flame (across the burner surface) sometimes continued briefly after the igniter was deactivated and retracted.  In one case on the first day of testing, there was excess fuel and a ‘fireball’ was ignited which promptly quenched.  Ignition also sometimes failed due to a software limitation where the fuelFlowIgnitionDeltaMs parameter which specifies the relative timing of the fuel flow and igniter activation must be evenly divisible by 100 (ms).  This limitation wasn’t initially understood and the igniter didn’t energize as a result. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

<mark>It was realized that the igniter for the burner gases could only be applied for up to 4 seconds, on insertion and retraction.  This produced an unanticipated problem, as the time for the burner gases to reach the igniter could be 10s of seconds depending on the set flow rate.  Also, after the first test, the burner would contain that fuel mixture minus diffusion with the chamber.  Thus the burner would need to be flushed with nitrogen before the second fuel mixture was initiated.</mark> 

<mark>Intense communication with the NASA team proceeded the day before the first test. The gas volume of the burner along with the feed pipe volume was supplied by NASA.  Accordingly ground experiments at Maryland were conducted with an identical NASA BRE burner along with its piping volume from the flow valve. These experiments and theory were used to develop an algorithm for determining the relative timing of the igniter and gas flow activation that will result in ignition.</mark> 

<mark>Day 1 was conducted with rough estimates made on the fly, and fortunately nearly all of the tests led to a satisfactory ignition.  By Day 2 an algorithm was established</mark> 

49 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>and successfully used for the remainder of the testing.  This algorithm will need to be refined and applied for the larger 50-mm diameter burner that will be used in Round 2.</mark> 

_<mark>A document on the development of the algorithm and its refinement for the larger burner is being</mark>_ _<u><mark>prepared and will be shared with the NASA team to facilitate Round 2.</mark></u>_ 

The second day of flame testing was delayed to allow for the development of a new ignition procedure, where concerns arose that the mixture of gas flowing from the burner wasn’t sufficiently well understood, i.e., in terms of concentrations.  This concern had to do with the burner’s large internal volume relative to that of the burners used for the other ACME experiments.  To be clear, this issue had to do with the analysis of results rather than the achievement of ignition.  Re-planning was also necessary early in BRE testing because the test durations were often much longer than had earlier been envisioned by the investigators.  While these factors didn’t directly interfere with the acquisition of test data, they did slow progress. 

While it rarely occurred, BRE tests sometimes automatically aborted (due to the controlling software script), where that is typically prior to gas flow and the activation of the igniter.  As a result, there is little lost other than the time associated with the failed test attempt.   Such aborts typically occur because of communication issues with an Image Processing and Storage Unit (IPSU). 

ACME’s Mass Flow Controllers (MFCs) used to control the flow of fuel and nitrogen have unfortunately not always performed adequately, where the flow rates have not always been appropriately steady.  The resulting effects on the flame behavior are tentatively assumed to be minor, but this still needs to be assessed. 

##### **C. Completeness/Quality of Data** 

The number of flames ignited significantly exceeds the planned tests – under the tested conditions (25-mm burner and ethylene as fuel) - which were specified as required in the ACME Integrated Science Requirements Document (ISRD).  A total of 25 such tests were required, while 45 flames were ignited in the Increment 58 portion of the experiment’s first round.  As such, roughly 80% more flames were ignited than had been planned even though the round hadn’t been completed. 

It should be understood that a complete set of image data is not necessarily available for all tests.  Recorded image data is sometimes deleted without downlinking.  This is done because of operational constraints which limit the amount of data that can be managed per test day.  Given the constraint, the decision is sometimes made to sacrifice some image data (e.g., from the HiBMs camera) to allow additional tests to be conducted.  This is intentionally done in order to maximize the scientific return of each day of testing within the project constraints. 

50 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **_From BRE Co-I Peter Sunderland:_** 

The video and image data of the testing produced satisfactory data to display the nature of the burning sequence from ignition to self-extinction or fuel termination. However, the image quality is less than was hoped for. The ACME ops camera provides video, but only offers 0.3 megapixels of resolution, and for many flames it is too zoomed out. The GigE camera has poor sensitivity in the blue, and after the ignition transient all our flames have been blue. This camera only offers 1.4 megapixel of resolution and requires about 1 s exposures for blue flames, which is far too slow to be considered video. The images provided by both cameras are, according to UMD marketing staff, at too low resolution to be used in press releases. 

Data quality is of course dependent on the experimental hardware.  But neglecting that limitation on data quality, the cameras are sensitive to radiation where pixels gradually become ‘hot’ over time.  While these bright spots are addressed in image post-processing, they are a degradation of the image quality. 

See discussion in III.D in regard to fouling or degradation of the paint on the BRE burner’s thermopile sensors. 

It is possible that the tests are subtly affected by the acceleration environment, but there is not obvious indication of this and it has thus far not been evaluated with available measurements from the Space Acceleration Measurement System (SAMS).  In this regard, note that the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), in which the experiment is conducted, is equipped with a Passive Rack Isolation System (PaRIS). 

Regardless of the shortcomings discussed above, a great wealth of experimental data has been secured and BRE’s first round (which ended in Increment 59) is considered to be successful despite the frequent saturation of the radiometer data discussed in section III.B.2. 

##### **D. Status of Data Analysis (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere)** 

<mark>All of Round 1 tests have had the thermopile sensor data reduced using their original calibrations.  As presented immediately below, the data must be continually processed to address the effect of the combustion product water and any paint degradation.</mark> 

<mark>The primary measurements are used, by two techniques, to obtain the net heat flux from the flame to the burner surface.  In the steady state, this net heat flux divided by the mass flow rate per unit area of the fuel mixture gives the heat of gasification of the emulated fuel.  The key hypothesis is that the heat of combustion, smoke point, and heat of gasification of the fuel mixture will represent a real solid fuel (or liquid fuel absorbed in an inert matrix).  Thus achieved steady burning would represent the potential for real solid fuels to achieve steady burning in microgravity.  Other than a candle flame, this has not been shown before.</mark> 

51 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>The first technique uses two thermopile heat flux gages embedded in the burner surface. One gage is at the center the other is offset from the center.  The resulting data suffered from two possible effects.   The first effect is the condensation of water vapor.  As the sensors are not cooled, but rise with temperature as the flame grows, water from its combustion products can condense and then evaporate from the sensor.  This occurs, as the dew point for combustion products is about 60 – 70</mark><sup>o</sup> <mark>C, and the sensor begins at ambient temperature and can achieve temperatures over 100</mark><sup>o</sup> <mark>C.  Thus condensation can occur at temperatures below the dew point that leads to added heat to the sensor.  Above the dew point, the condensed water will evaporate and reduce the heat to the sensor. Once the sensor is dry, it will only measure the heat flux from the flame gases as intended.</mark> 

<mark>We have duplicated this condensation effect in 1-g laboratory experiments with our duplicative NASA BRE burner.  The figure below (Fig. 1) shows an illustration of the condensation and evaporation effect in the ISS tests.   The offset sensor in red shows a clear jump increase when the water fully evaporates.    More analysis is needed to properly correct for this effect.  Fortunately by the end of the test shown here (and for all of the other data), this water effect is gone.</mark> 

<mark>Figure 1.  Typical net heat flux of thermopile sensors</mark> 

<mark>The second effect on the thermopile sensors is that the heat may have fouled their paint. This could then affect their calibration.  Figure 2 shows an enlargement of the burner after Round 1 tests were concluded.   It can be seen that the paint on the sensors is degraded. This was the original manufacturer’s paint, instead of that used for the copper top.  The copper paint appears fine.  We are working with the thermopile manufacturer to explore the effect of heat damage on the calibration of the thermopile sensors.  This will give us a way to determine a quantitative change in the calibration.</mark> 

52 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>Figure 2.  Paint degradation on thermopile sensors</mark> 

<mark>The second technique to determine the heat flux is the copper calorimeter forming the top of the burner.  The temperature rise of the copper is key to that determination.  The next figure (Fig. 3) shows that typically the copper temperature is nearly uniform and smooth in time.  It does not appear that condensation or evaporation is having any effect on its temperature behavior.  So we believe we are justified in using this measurement to predict the overall average heat flux from the flame to the burner.  However, we do need to evaluate any possible effect of the combustion product water.  In addition, these calorimeter data are not fully processed yet because we are refining our methodology for its calibration.  If the thermopile data cannot be completely used with good accuracy, we have the calorimeter to fall back on.</mark> 

53 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

Figure 3.  Copper and heat flux sensor temperatures, Day 6, D1 

<mark>Preliminary analysis of the radiometer sensor data has indicated its consistent ability to measure the flame overall radiation heat loss fraction.  The method to determine the radiative heat flux component to the burner is more complex, and it will rely on theoretical modeling.  This will take some time.</mark> 

<mark>The video images of all the flames have been compiled, and are being quantified in term of shape and dimensions.</mark> 

<mark>Alexander Snegirev, our Russian partner, has attempted a numerical simulation of at least one of the ISS BRE tests.  That work is still in progress.  It should be remarked his student won the Student Prize for Best Presentation at the 9</mark><sup>th</sup> <mark>International Seminar on Fire and Explosion Hazards, St. Petersburg, April 2019 for simulating our 5-s BRE drop tests.</mark> 

##### **E. Preliminary Research Findings (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere)** 

<mark>Despite having much data to digest and completely analyze, there is a profound conclusion from these tests.  The burner has definitively shown that steady burning is possible for real solids with a flat surface of 25 mm diameter.  Up to now most flammability studies in microgravity have focused on flame spread with a wind for concurrent spread and at least a self-induced wind for opposed flow spread.  We already knew that this would be possible from 1-g research.   The burning of liquids, as in a wick of a candle flame and small droplets, has been shown to produce steady flames. However, in the case of droplet burning cool flames could sustain normal burning, and normal burning would extinguish.  The extinction has been shown to result from the radiative cooling of the flame.  We see this as well in the extended ISS microgravity tests. However this extinction was predominately seen for atmospheric conditions of 21 %</mark> 

54 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>oxygen and 14.7 psia.  At conditions of 27 % oxygen, 10.2 psia and 34 % oxygen, 8.2 psia, the flames mostly persisted for 2 minutes or more as steady.  These are all atmospheric conditions that NASA may use.  Some examples will be shown to illustrate results over these atmospheres.</mark> 













2 Ethylene/N2,   _hc_ = 22 kJ/g,  4.55 g/m s,  27% O2, 10.2 psia, _L_ = 0.66 kJ/g, extinction @ 45 s 

<mark>Figure 4.  Day 3, Test F1.  Flame growth from ignition to self-extinction</mark> 

<mark>Figure 4 shows a typical test of ignition and flame growth.  Although extinction occurred at 45 s with the flame still growing, the heat flux was nearly steady by 30 s.  Therefore a heat of gasification (</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>) could be estimated.  This was done in a preliminary estimation by using the center heat flux sensor as the net average heat flux.  If the heating is just due to diffusion, theory indicates that the average heat flux is double the center value.  Hence such an estimated heat of gasification could be low by 50 %.  On the other hand, if the sensor is degraded in its calibration and radiation is a significant component of the heat flux, then the estimate will also be low.  Hence, using the center heat flux sensor directly gives a conservatively low estimate of the heat of gasification in the emulation of real solids.  This will be significant when the totality of results for the 25 mm flat burning surface is display over its spectrum of conservatively low values for</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>.</mark> 

<mark>Figures 5 and 6 illustrate results for atmospheric conditions of 26.5 % oxygen and 0.7 atm (10.2 psia), and 34 % oxygen and 0.56 atm (8.2 psia), respectively.  Both use 50 % mixtures of nitrogen and ethylene giving a fuel mixture heat of combustion of 21.8 kJ/g.  Both of these tests reached steady conditions and were terminated by shutting off the fuel.</mark>

## **30-day Operational Accomplishments Report** 

**… for the International Space Station (ISS) Increments 59 and 60** 

**ACME Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments BRE Burning Rate Emulator** 

_BRE is one of several independent experiments that are part of the ACME project, which uses a single modular set of hardware to carry out studies of flames burning gaseous fuels in the ISS’ Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR).  The investigation will be carried out in two separate rounds, where the first round began in Increment 58 and was completed in Increment 59, so there is nothing to be reported for Increment 60.  The second round of testing will be conducted in Increments 61 and/or 62._ 

Prepared by the PI team and Dennis Stocker (dennis.p.stocker@nasa.gov), the ACME Project Scientist, who is the primary contact for BRE and ACME information. 

Principal Investigator (PI): Prof. James G. Quintiere, University of Maryland, College Park, MD Co-Investigator (Co-I): Prof. Peter B. #### **I. Introduction** 

The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment is conducted in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station, as part of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project.  BRE is focused on spacecraft fire prevention.  More specifically, BRE’s goals are to improve our fundamental understanding of materials flammability, such as extinction behavior and the conditions needed for sustained combustion, and to assess the relevance of existing flammability test methods for low and partial-gravity environments.  A flat porous burner fed with gaseous fuel simulates the burning of solid and liquid fuels, where measurements are made of the thermal feedback (i.e., to the burner) upon which the vaporization of condensed-phase fuels is dependent.  A small number of gaseous fuels (including mixtures with inert gases) are used to simulate the burning of fuels such as paper, plastic, and 

60 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

alcohol by matching properties including the heats of combustion and gasification, the surface temperature, and smoke point.   The experiment’s results can establish fuel properties that directly relate to fire hazards in spacecraft, and if effective in microgravity could provide an efficient, safe, and comprehensive technique for assessing flammability and selecting fireresistant spacecraft materials. 

BRE’s specific experimental objectives are as follows, where it must be emphasized that NASA does not currently plan to carry out the desired tests for objective B. 

- D. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25 and 50 mm diameter. Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures. NASA-considered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen. Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels. 

- E. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambients of O2/CO2 (desired). 

- F. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits. 

The observations will enable quantification of the microgravity behavior of condensed fuels (i.e., liquids and non-charring plastics). 

Microgravity studies of flames allow for a range of simplifications and provide various advantages over normal-gravity studies.  For example, the flicker of flame, which is a buoyancy-driven (i.e., gravity-driven) instability, is eliminated yielding quasi-steady flames in microgravity.  Length scales are increased in microgravity flames facilitating analysis of the flame structure. Momentum-dominated flames, which are important for most practical combustion, can be studied at low velocities to simplify analysis.  Microgravity flames tend to have a much stronger sensitivity to their atmosphere and exhibit a much broader range of characteristics than normalgravity flames because of the near absence of buoyant entrainment.  The long residence times in microgravity flames can lead to strong soot production, but many microgravity flames are soot free.  The absence of buoyancy makes microgravity flames advantageous for studies of limit and stability behavior where chemical kinetics are important.  With these advantages, microgravity testing can lead to new understanding of the complex nature of combustion – regardless of the gravitational environment of the combustion application. 

#### **II. Methods/Research Operations** 

##### **A. Discussion of Method/Protocol** 

BRE and other ACME experiments are configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew. The crew’s role is to set up the hardware, gas bottles, etc. for each set of tests.  The most common changes include the gas bottles on the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) and the mass flow controllers on the modular ACME chamber insert. 

61 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

All testing is commanded from the ground, specifically from the operations center at the NASA Glenn Research Center.  The science portion of the testing is typically conducted in a nominally automated mode using pre-programmed scripts.  However, it may alternately be conducted in a ‘manual’ mode, where changes are made to a control parameter in response to the analog video downlink and the numerical data available in the telemetry stream.  For example, gas flow rate(s) can be adjusted after quasi-steady data has been collected.  But testing has not been conducted in a ‘manual’ mode thus far for BRE or ACME in general. 

Testing is not conducted when thrusters fire or spacecraft dock or undock from ISS to ensure an acceptable acceleration environment. 

Data is downlinked between each set of tests, i.e., day of testing, to allow for preliminary analysis and requisite planning for subsequent testing. 

#### **III. Results** 

##### **A. Baselined Activities Completed – Pre-flight, In-flight, Post-Flight** 

The first round of BRE tests began in February 2019, where a total of 45 flames were ignited during Increment 58.  That round was completed in April 2019, during the beginning of Increment 59, when an additional 17 flames were ignited.  As such, a total of 62 flames were ignited in the first round of testing.  Meanwhile, the second round of BRE testing is expected to begin near the end of Increment 61 and continue into Increment 62.  The ‘interruption’ in the BRE testing was intentionally planned years in advance to enable the PI team to review the initial results in detail and with that knowledge plan the second round of tests to maximize its scientific outcome. 

All of the first round of tests were conducted with the 25-mm diameter BRE burner while burning ethylene (C2H4) - sometimes diluted with nitrogen.  The tests were carried out at a range of chamber pressures and oxygen concentrations, generally representative of possible crew environments in future spacecraft or habitats.  More specifically, testing was especially focused on atmospheric conditions at (1) 14.7 psia, 21% oxygen; (2) 10.2 psia, 26.5% oxygen; and (3) 8.2 psia, 34% oxygen.  In all cases, the atmosphere was diluted with nitrogen and any incomplete combustion products (e.g., water vapor and carbon dioxide) from preceding tests.  An exception is tests conducted after the chamber atmosphere was vented and refilled, such as the first test of the day.  But the atmosphere was typically refreshed once during each of testing. 

BRE does not have living subjects like a life science experiment. The tests are independently conducted with two different gaseous fuels, methane and ethylene.  Both are simple hydrocarbon gases for which the chemistry is well understood.  Methane (CH4) is the primary component of natural gas and tends to produce low levels of soot given the absence of carbon-carbon bonds.  Meanwhile, ethylene (C2H4), is normally highly sooting 

62 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

because of its carbon double bond.  Tests are conducted with pure fuel and fuel mixed with nitrogen, where the fuel and nitrogen was premixed on the ground prior to launch for some cases.  But the ACME hardware also allows for simultaneous flow of fuel and nitrogen to the burner, allowing for on-orbit fuel dilution and testing at a continuum of fuel concentrations. 

There are no samples (e.g., of soot) collected for BRE, where all of the ISS results are electronically transmitted to the ground.  That is furthermore the case for all currently planned ACME experiments. 

A summary log of the flames ignited for BRE during the first round of testing - in Increments 58 and 59 - is found in Table 1.  The table includes a limited overview of the test conditions.  For completeness, a row was included for testing on April 1 (GMT 091) even though it was quickly discovered that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed at the end of the preceding test day.  As a result, flames were not ignited until a few days later after astronaut Nick Hague had replaced the igniter tip.

##### **B. List of Preflight, In-flight, Postflight Anomalies** 

**1. Mission anomalies** 

There were no significant anomalies hindering BRE operations during Increment 59, but there was one in Increment 58 during the preparations for BRE testing began (that was mistakenly not addressed in the 30-day report including that increment).  It was discovered on Feb. 4 (GMT 035) that the pressure was low in a check gas bottle for the CIR’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentation Package (GCIP).  The bottle had been on the space station for 9 years, and the low pressure was attributed to a nominal leak rate.  The check gas was for calibration of the GCIP measurements and not needed for regular operations which proceeded unhindered.  The bottle was replaced on June 13 to enable the necessary calibration. 

**2. Any other difficulties in obtaining scientific data** 

BRE’s primary problem was that the sensors for measuring the thermal radiation often saturated.  Although the measurements were not required to meet experiment success, they were considered sufficiently critical that BRE was equipped with its own radiometer array, featuring three thermopile detectors.  Estimates had been made of the signal levels based on 5-second drop tests since the hot gases tend to blanket the flame in the absence of buoyancy and thereby remain within the view of the radiometers.  As a result, the signal levels are strongly dependent on gravity.  The estimates were not part of the science requirements and may not have been sufficiently tracked in the hardware development.  Furthermore, the ISS tests lasted as long as 5 minutes, which is over four times the envisioned BRE test duration and 60 times the duration of the drop tests.  With the fixed amplification of the radiometer signals, the data was frequently lost - other than the knowledge that the limits were exceeded.  An engineering review failed to identify an acceptable solution and BRE testing continued unchanged. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

The radiometry array proved problematic as its sensors were mostly out of range and quickly became saturated.  However, for some of the small size flames, these data were complete.  Those data will be vital to assessing the role of flame and combustion product radiation. 

Ignition was not always successful.  As previously discussed, it was discovered on April 1 (GMT 091) that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed on the preceding test day, preventing further flame testing until it could be replaced.  While such circumstances are uncommon for ACME operations, BRE tests were sometimes conducted under conditions, e.g., for igniter activation and deactivation, which led to momentary flashes without sustained ignition.  Given that unplanned crew time is required to replace failed igniter tips, the BRE ops team limited - to varying degrees - the igniter voltage and the time that it was energized within the flame.  The latter was known from earlier research in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) to degrade the hot-wire 

64 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

igniter tip and hasten its demise.  It is notable that the flashes of flame (across the burner surface) sometimes continued briefly after the igniter was deactivated and retracted.  In one case on the first day of testing, there was excess fuel and a ‘fireball’ was ignited which promptly quenched.  Ignition also sometimes failed due to a software limitation where the fuelFlowIgnitionDeltaMs parameter which specifies the relative timing of the fuel flow and igniter activation must be evenly divisible by 100 (ms).  This limitation wasn’t initially understood and the igniter didn’t energize as a result. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

<mark>It was realized that the igniter for the burner gases could only be applied for up to 4 seconds, on insertion and retraction.  This produced an unanticipated problem, as the time for the burner gases to reach the igniter could be tens of seconds depending on the flow rate.  Also, after the first test, the burner would contain that fuel mixture mixed with the chamber gas.  Thus the burner would need to be flushed with nitrogen before the second fuel mixture was initiated.</mark> 

<mark>Intense communication with the NASA team proceeded the day before the first test. The gas volume of the burner along with the feed pipe volume was supplied by NASA.  Accordingly ground experiments at UMD were conducted with an identical NASA BRE burner along with its piping volume from the flow valve. These experiments and theory were used to develop an algorithm for determining the relative timing of the igniter and gas flow activation that will result in ignition.</mark> 

<mark>Day 1 was conducted with rough estimates made on the fly, and fortunately nearly all of the tests led to a satisfactory ignition.  By Day 2 an algorithm was established and successfully used for the remainder of the testing.  This algorithm will need to be refined and applied for the larger 50 mm diameter burner that will be used in Round 2.</mark> 

_<mark>A document on the development of the algorithm and its refinement for the larger burner is being</mark>_ _<u><mark>prepared and will be shared with the NASA team to facilitate round 2.</mark></u>_ 

The second day of flame testing was delayed to allow for the development of a new ignition procedure, where concerns arose that the mixture of gas flowing from the burner wasn’t sufficiently well understood, i.e., in terms of concentrations.  This concern had to do with the burner’s large internal volume relative to that of the burners used for the other ACME experiments.  To be clear, this issue had to do with the analysis of results rather than the achievement of ignition.  Re-planning was also necessary early in BRE testing because the test durations were often much longer than had earlier been envisioned by the investigators.  While these factors didn’t directly interfere with the acquisition of test data, they did slow progress. 

65 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

While it rarely occurred, BRE tests sometimes automatically aborted (due to the controlling software script), where that is typically prior to gas flow and the activation of the igniter.  As a result, there is little lost other than the time associated with the failed test attempt.   Such aborts typically occur because of communication issues with an Image Processing and Storage Unit (IPSU). 

ACME’s Mass Flow Controllers (MFCs) used to control the flow of fuel and nitrogen have unfortunately not always performed adequately, where the flow rates have not always been appropriately steady.  The resulting effects on the flame behavior are tentatively assumed to be minor, but this still needs to be assessed. 

##### **C. Completeness/Quality of Data** 

The number of flames ignited in Increments 58 and 59 significantly exceeds the planned tests – under the tested conditions (25 mm burner and ethylene as fuel) - which were specified as required in the ACME Integrated Science Requirements Document (ISRD).  A total of 25 such tests were required, while 62 flames with those conditions were ignited. As such, more than twice as many flames were ignited as had been planned. 

It should be understood that a complete set of image data is not necessarily available for all tests.  Recorded image data is sometimes deleted without downlinking.  This is done because of operational constraints which limit the amount of data that can be managed per test day.  Given the constraint, the decision is sometimes made to sacrifice some image data (e.g., from the HiBMs camera) to allow additional tests to be conducted.  This is intentionally done to maximize the scientific return of each day of testing within the project constraints. 

###### **_From BRE Co-I Peter Sunderland:_** 

The video and image data of the testing produced satisfactory data to display the nature of the burning sequence from ignition to self-extinction or fuel termination. However, the image quality is less than was hoped for. The ACME ops camera provides video, but only offers 0.3 megapixels of resolution, and for many flames it is too zoomed out. The GigE camera has poor sensitivity in the blue, and after the ignition transient all our flames have been blue. This camera only offers 1.4 megapixels of resolution and requires about 1 s exposures for blue flames, which is far too slow to be considered video. The images provided by both cameras are, according to UMD marketing staff, at too low resolution to be used in press releases. In round 2 we will increase the gain on the GigE camera, an option we were unaware of in round 1. 

Data quality is of course dependent on the experimental hardware.  But neglecting that limitation on data quality, the cameras are sensitive to radiation where pixels gradually become ‘hot’ over time.  While these bright spots are addressed in image post-processing, they are a degradation of the image quality. 

66 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

See discussion in III.D in regard to fouling or degradation of the paint on the BRE burner’s heat flux sensors. 

It is possible that the tests are subtly affected by the acceleration environment, but there is not obvious indication of this and it has thus far not been evaluated with available measurements from the Space Acceleration Measurement System (SAMS).  In this regard, note that the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), in which the experiment is conducted, is equipped with a Passive Rack Isolation System (PaRIS). 

Regardless of the shortcomings discussed above, a great wealth of experimental data has been secured and BRE’s first round, completed in Increment 59, is considered to be successful despite the frequent saturation of the radiometer data discussed in section III.B.2. 

##### **D. Status of Data Analysis (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student Parham Dehghani)** 

All measured transient raw data (e.g., heat flux sensors, thermocouples, radiometers, mass flow meters and chamber pressure sensor) have been converted to engineering units and plotted using the calibrations provided in ACME’s Software Interface Design Description (ACME-DOC-010 Rev. D). 

All of the round 1 tests have had the heat flux sensor data reduced using their original calibrations.  As presented below, the data is being processed to address the effect of the combustion product water and paint degradation. 

The primary measurements are used, by two techniques, to obtain the net heat flux from the flame to the burner surface.  In the steady state, this net heat flux divided by the mass flow rate per unit area of the fuel mixture gives the heat of gasification of the emulated fuel.  The key hypothesis is that the heat of combustion, smoke point, and heat of gasification of the fuel mixture will represent a real solid fuel (or liquid fuel absorbed in an inert matrix).  Thus, achieved steady burning would represent the potential for real solid fuels to achieve steady burning in microgravity.  Other than for a candle flame, this has not been shown before. 

The first technique uses two thermopile heat flux gages embedded in the burner surface. One gage is at the center the other is offset from the center.  The resulting data suffered from two possible effects.   The first effect is the condensation of water vapor.  As the sensors are not cooled, but rise with temperature as the flame grows, water from its combustion products can condense and then evaporate from the sensor.  This occurs, as the dew point for combustion products is about 60-70<sup>o</sup> C, and the sensor begins at ambient temperature and can achieve temperatures over 100<sup>o</sup> C.  Thus, condensation can occur at temperatures below the dew point that leads to added heat to the sensor.  Above the dew point, the condensed water will evaporate and reduce the heat to the sensor. 

67 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

When the sensor is dry and above 100<sup>o</sup> C, it will only measure the heat flux from the flame gases as intended. 

We have duplicated this condensation effect in 1-g laboratory experiments with our duplicative NASA BRE burner.  Fig. 1 illustrates the condensation and evaporation effect in the ISS tests.   The offset sensor in red shows a clear jump increase when the water fully evaporates.    More analysis is needed to properly correct for this effect.  Fortunately, by the end of the test shown here (and for all of the other data), this water effect is gone. 

**<mark>Figure 1.</mark>** <mark>Typical net heat flux of the heat flux sensors.</mark> 

<mark>The second effect on the heat flux sensors is that the heat may have fouled their paint. This could then affect their calibration.  Figure 2 shows an image of the ISS burner after round 1 tests were concluded.   It can be seen that the paint on the sensors is degraded. This was the original manufacturer’s paint, instead of that used for the copper top.  The copper paint appears fine.  We are working with the heat flux gage manufacturer to explore the effect of heat damage on the calibration of the sensors.  This will give us a way to determine a quantitative change in the calibration.</mark> 

68 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 



**<mark>Figure 2.</mark>** <mark>Paint degradation on heat flux sensors.</mark> 

<mark>The second technique to determine the heat flux is the copper calorimeter forming the top of the burner.  The temperature rise of the copper is key to that determination. Figure 3 shows that typically the copper temperature is nearly uniform and smooth in time.  It does not appear that condensation or evaporation is having any effect on its temperature behavior.  So, we believe we are justified in using this measurement to predict the overall average heat flux from the flame to the burner.  However, we do need to evaluate any possible effect of the combustion product water.  In addition, these calorimeter data are not fully processed yet because we are refining our methodology for its calibration.  If the heat flux data cannot be completely used with good accuracy, we have the calorimeter to fall back on.</mark>

The calorimeter represents the average heat flux to the surface and is being used to obtain heats of gasification.  Figure 4 shows the transient plots of test 19066D5. ##### **E. Preliminary Research Findings (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student Parham Dehghani)** 

<mark>Despite having much data to digest and analyze, there is a profound conclusion from these tests.  The burner has definitively shown that steady burning is possible for real solids with a flat surface of 25 mm diameter.  Up to now most flammability studies in microgravity have focused on flame spread with a wind for concurrent spread and at least a self-induced wind for opposed flow spread.  We already knew that this would be possible from normal-gravity research.   The burning of liquids, as in a wick of a candle flame and small droplets, has been shown to produce steady flames.  However, many droplet flames were found to extinguish owing to radiative cooling of the flame.  We see this as well in the extended ISS microgravity tests.  However, this extinction was predominately seen for atmospheric conditions of 21% oxygen and 14.7 psia.  At conditions of 27% oxygen, 10.2 psia and 34% oxygen, 8.2 psia, the flames generally persisted for 2 minutes or more.  These are all atmospheric conditions that NASA is considering for future spacecraft.  Some examples are shown below to illustrate these results.</mark> 

<mark>Figure 5 shows a typical test of ignition and flame growth.  Although extinction occurred at 45 s with the flame still growing, the heat flux was nearly steady by 30 s.  Therefore, a heat of gasification (</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>) could be estimated.  This was done in a preliminary estimation by using the center heat flux sensor as the net average heat flux.  If the heating is just due to diffusion, theory indicates that the average heat flux is double the center value.  Hence such an estimated heat of gasification could be low by 50%.  On the other hand, if the sensor is degraded in its calibration and radiation is a significant component of the heat flux, then the estimate will also be low.  Hence, using the center heat flux sensor directly gives a conservatively low estimate of the heat of gasification in the emulation of real solids.  This will be significant when the totality of results for the 25 mm flat burning surface is displayed over its spectrum of conservatively low values for</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>.</mark> 

72 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022

Both use 50% mixtures of nitrogen and ethylene giving a fuel mixture heat of combustion of 21.8 kJ/g.  Both of these tests reached steady conditions and were terminated by shutting off the fuel.</mark>

**<mark>Figure 12.</mark>** <mark>Radiative loss fraction vs. measured flame height (a) and flame volume (b).</mark> 

<mark>Two methods are proposed to obtain radiative heat flux on the burner surface. The first method is called ‘Mean Beam Length’ method and it is assumed that the radiative gasses are distributed homogeneously throughout the flame envelope.  The second method is called the ‘View Factor’ method and it assumes that the flame is a thin sheet where all the radiation is generated at this sheet.  Radiation view factor of the surface of the burner with respect to the flame sheet is then calculated which leads to obtaining the radiative heat flux on the burner surface.  Figure 13 shows the radiative heat flux on the burner surface obtained by mentioned methods.</mark>

12 December2020 

**30-day Operational Accomplishments Report … for the International Space Station (ISS) Increment 63** 

#### **ACME Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments BRE Burning Rate Emulator** 

_BRE is one of several independent experiments that are part of the ACME project, which uses a single modular set of hardware to carry out studies of flames burning gaseous fuels in the ISS’ Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR).  The investigation is being carried out in two separate rounds, where the first round was conducted during Increments 58 and 59 and the second round began in Increment 63 and should be completed in Increment 64._ 

Prepared by the PI team and Dennis Stocker (dennis.p.stocker@nasa.gov), the ACME Project Scientist, who is the primary contact for BRE and ACME information. 

|Principal Investigator (PI):|Prof. James G. Quintiere, University of Maryland, College Park, MD|
|---|---|
|Co-Investigator (Co-I):|Prof. Peter B. Sunderland, U. of Maryland, College Park, MD|
|Co-Investigator (Co-I):|Dr. John L. de Ris, FM Global (retired)|
|Collaborator:|Prof. Alexander Yu. Snegirev, Peter the Great St. Petersburg|
||Polytechnic University,St. Petersburg, Russia|
|Project Scientist (PS):|Dennis P. Stocker, NASA Glenn Research Center, Cleveland, OH|
|PS team member:|Dr. Uday G. Hegde, Case Western Reserve University,Cleveland, OH|



#### **I. Introduction** 

The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment is conducted in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station, as part of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project.  BRE is focused on spacecraft fire prevention.  More specifically, BRE’s goals are to improve our fundamental understanding of materials flammability, such as extinction behavior and the conditions needed for sustained combustion, and to assess the relevance of existing flammability test methods for low and partial-gravity environments.  A flat porous burner fed with gaseous fuel simulates the burning of solid and liquid fuels, where measurements are made of the thermal feedback (i.e., to the burner) upon which the vaporization of condensed-phase fuels is dependent.  A small number of gaseous fuels (including mixtures with inert gases) are used to simulate the burning of fuels such as paper, plastic, and alcohol by matching properties including the heats of combustion and gasification, the surface 

79 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

temperature, and smoke point.   The experiment’s results can establish fuel properties that directly relate to fire hazards in spacecraft, and if effective in microgravity could provide an efficient, safe, and comprehensive technique for assessing flammability and selecting fireresistant spacecraft materials. 

BRE’s specific experimental objectives are as follows, where it must be emphasized that NASA does not currently plan to carry out the desired tests for objective B. 

- G. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25 and 50 mm diameter. Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures. NASA-considered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen. Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels. 

- H. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambients of O2/CO2 (desired). 

- I. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits. 

The observations will enable quantification of the microgravity behavior of condensed fuels (i.e., liquids and non-charring plastics). 

Microgravity studies of flames allow for a range of simplifications and provide various advantages over normal-gravity studies.  For example, the flicker of flame, which is a buoyancy-driven (i.e., gravity-driven) instability, is eliminated yielding quasi-steady flames in microgravity.  Length scales are increased in microgravity flames facilitating analysis of the flame structure. Momentum-dominated flames, which are important for most practical combustion, can be studied at low velocities to simplify analysis.  Microgravity flames tend to have a much stronger sensitivity to their atmosphere and exhibit a much broader range of characteristics than normalgravity flames because of the near absence of buoyant entrainment.  The long residence times in microgravity flames can lead to strong soot production, but many microgravity flames are soot free.  The absence of buoyancy makes microgravity flames advantageous for studies of limit and stability behavior where chemical kinetics are important.  With these advantages, microgravity testing can lead to new understanding of the complex nature of combustion – regardless of the gravitational environment of the combustion application. 

#### **II. Methods/Research Operations** 

##### **A. Discussion of Method/Protocol** 

BRE and other ACME experiments are configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew. The crew’s role is to set up the hardware, gas bottles, etc. for each set of tests.  The most common changes include the gas bottles on the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) and the mass flow controllers on the modular ACME chamber insert. 

All testing is commanded from the ground, specifically from the operations center at the NASA Glenn Research Center.  The science portion of the testing is typically conducted in 

80 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

a nominally automated mode using pre-programmed scripts.  However, it could alternately be conducted in a ‘manual’ mode, where changes are made to a control parameter in response to the analog video downlink and the numerical data available in the telemetry stream.  For example, gas flow rate(s) can be adjusted after quasi-steady data has been collected.  But testing has not been conducted in a ‘manual’ mode thus far for BRE or ACME in general. 

Testing is not conducted when thrusters fire or spacecraft dock or undock from ISS to ensure an acceptable acceleration environment. 

Data is downlinked between each set of tests, i.e., day of testing, to allow for preliminary analysis and requisite planning for subsequent testing. 

#### **III. Results** 

##### **A. Baselined Activities Completed – Pre-flight, In-flight, Post-Flight** 

The first round of BRE tests began in February 2019, during Increment 58, when a total of 45 flames were ignited.  That round was completed in April 2019, during Increment 59, when an additional 17 flames were ignited.  As such, a total of 62 flames were ignited in the first round of testing.  Meanwhile, the BRE’s second-round flame testing began in Sept. 2020, during Increment 63, during which 47 flames were ignited.  The second and final round of tests should be completed during Increment 64. 

The ‘interruption’ in the BRE testing was intentionally planned years in advance to enable the PI team to review the initial results in detail and with that knowledge plan the second round of tests to maximize its scientific outcome.  However, the break was longer than had been planned because of combination of hardware issues and limited crew availability.  As a result, BRE’s second round was rescheduled to occur after both rounds of the preceding ACME experiment, Structure and Response of Spherical Diffusion Flames (s-Flame), was completed, where that occurred in July 2020.  A factor in that decision was the delayed start of commercial crew launches and understanding that crew availability would be limited until such crew launches entered an operational, rather than demonstration, phase. 

All first-round tests were conducted with the 25-mm diameter BRE burner while burning ethylene (C2H4) - sometimes diluted with nitrogen.  The tests were carried out at a range of chamber pressures and oxygen concentrations, generally representative of possible crew environments in future spacecraft or habitats.  More specifically, testing was especially focused on atmospheric conditions at (1) 14.7 psia, 21% oxygen; (2) 10.2 psia, 26.5% oxygen; and (3) 8.2 psia, 34% oxygen.  In all cases, the atmosphere was diluted with nitrogen and any incomplete combustion products (e.g., water vapor and carbon dioxide) from preceding tests.  An exception is tests conducted after the chamber atmosphere was 

81 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

vented and refilled, such as the first test of the day.  But during tests days, the atmosphere was typically refreshed once or twice between sets of tests. 

Second round testing is being carried out including both BRE burner diameters, i.e., 25 and 50 mm, and on some days with methane (CH4) – again sometimes diluted with nitrogen.  Therefore, the second round of tests features a broader range of conditions than investigated during the first round. 

BRE does not have living subjects like a life science experiment. The tests are independently conducted with two different gaseous fuels, methane and ethylene.  Both are simple hydrocarbon gases for which the chemistry is well understood.  Methane (CH4) is the primary component of natural gas and tends to produce low levels of soot given the absence of carbon-carbon bonds.  Meanwhile, ethylene (C2H4), is normally highly sooting because of its carbon double bond.  Tests are conducted with pure fuel and fuel mixed with nitrogen, where the fuel and nitrogen were premixed on the ground prior to launch for some cases.  But the ACME hardware also allows for simultaneous flow of fuel and nitrogen to the burner, allowing for on-orbit fuel dilution and testing at a continuum of fuel concentrations. 

There are no samples (e.g., of soot) collected for BRE, where all ISS results are electronically transmitted to the ground.  That is furthermore the case for all currently planned ACME experiments. 

A summary log of the flames ignited for BRE during Increments 58 and 59 (the first round of tests) and Increment 63 (the start of second round) is found in Table 1.  The table includes a limited overview of the test conditions.  For completeness, a row was included for testing on April 1 (GMT 091) even though it was quickly discovered that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed at the end of the preceding test day.  As a result, flames were not ignited until a few days later after astronaut Nick Hague had replaced the igniter tip.

##### **B. List of Preflight, In-flight, Postflight Anomalies** 

###### **1. Mission anomalies** 

There were no significant anomalies hindering BRE operations during Increments 59 and 63, but there was one in Increment 58 during the preparations for BRE testing (that was mistakenly not addressed in the 30-day report including that increment).  It was discovered on Feb. 4 (GMT 035) that the pressure was low in a check gas bottle for the CIR’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentation Package (GCIP).  The bottle had been on the space station for 9 years, and the low pressure was attributed to a nominal leak rate.  The check gas was for calibration of the GCIP measurements and not needed for regular operations which proceeded unhindered.  The bottle was replaced on June 13 to enable the necessary calibration. 

83 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **2. Any other difficulties in obtaining scientific data** 

BRE’s primary problem was that the sensors for measuring the thermal radiation often saturated.  Although the measurements were not required to meet experiment success, they were considered sufficiently critical that BRE was equipped with its own radiometer array, featuring three thermopile detectors.  Estimates had been made of the signal levels based on 5-second drop tests since the hot gases tend to blanket the flame in the absence of buoyancy and thereby remain within the view of the radiometers.  As a result, the signal levels are strongly dependent on gravity.  The estimates were not part of the science requirements and may not have been sufficiently tracked in the hardware development.  Furthermore, the ISS tests lasted as long as 5 minutes, which is over four times the envisioned BRE test duration and 60 times the duration of the drop tests.  With the fixed amplification of the radiometer signals, the data was frequently lost - other than the knowledge that the limits were exceeded.  An engineering review failed to identify an acceptable solution and BRE testing continued unchanged. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

The radiometry array proved problematic as its sensors were mostly out of range and quickly became saturated.  However, for some of the small size flames, these data were complete.  Those data will be vital to assessing the role of flame and combustion product radiation. 

Ignition was not always successful.  As previously discussed, it was discovered on 1 April 2019 (GMT 091) that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed on the preceding test day, preventing further flame testing until it could be replaced.  While such circumstances are uncommon for ACME operations, BRE tests were sometimes conducted under conditions, e.g., for igniter activation and deactivation, which led to momentary flashes without sustained ignition.  Given that unplanned crew time is required to replace failed igniter tips, the BRE ops team limited - to varying degrees - the igniter voltage and the time that it was energized within the flame.  The latter was known from earlier research in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) to degrade the hot-wire igniter tip and hasten its demise.  It is notable that the flashes of flame (across the burner surface) sometimes continued briefly after the igniter was deactivated and retracted.  In one case on the first day of testing, there was excess fuel and a ‘fireball’ was ignited which promptly quenched.  Ignition also sometimes failed due to a software limitation where the fuelFlowIgnitionDeltaMs parameter which specifies the relative timing of the fuel flow and igniter activation must be evenly divisible by 100 (ms).  This limitation wasn’t initially understood and the igniter didn’t energize as a result. 

84 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

<mark>It was realized that the igniter for the burner gases could only be applied for up to 4 seconds, on insertion and retraction.  This produced an unanticipated problem, as the time for the burner gases to reach the igniter could be tens of seconds depending on the flow rate.  Also, after the first test, the burner would contain that fuel mixture mixed with the chamber gas.  Thus the burner would need to be flushed with nitrogen before the second fuel mixture was initiated.</mark> 

<mark>Intense communication with the NASA team proceeded the day before the first test. The gas volume of the burner along with the feed pipe volume was supplied by NASA.  Accordingly ground experiments at UMD were conducted with an identical NASA BRE burner along with its piping volume from the flow valve. These experiments and theory were used to develop an algorithm for determining the relative timing of the igniter and gas flow activation that will result in ignition.</mark> 

<mark>Day 1 [of the first round] was conducted with rough estimates made on the fly, and fortunately nearly all of the tests led to a satisfactory ignition.  By Day 2 an algorithm was established and successfully used for the remainder of the testing. This algorithm will need to be refined and applied for the larger 50 mm diameter burner that will be used in Round 2.</mark> 

<mark>A document on the development of the algorithm and its refinement for the larger burner [was] prepared and … shared with the NASA team to facilitate [ignition in] round 2.</mark> 

The second day of round-1 flame testing was delayed to allow for the development of a new ignition procedure, where concerns arose that the mixture of gas flowing from the burner wasn’t sufficiently well understood, i.e., in terms of concentrations.  This concern had to do with the burner’s large internal volume relative to that of the burners used for the other ACME experiments.  To be clear, this issue had to do with the analysis of results rather than the achievement of ignition.  Re-planning was also necessary early in BRE testing because the test durations were often much longer than had earlier been envisioned by the investigators.  While these factors didn’t directly interfere with the acquisition of test data, they did slow progress. 

While it rarely occurred, BRE tests sometimes automatically aborted (due to the controlling software script), where that is typically prior to gas flow and the activation of the igniter.  As a result, there is little lost other than the time associated with the failed test attempt.   Such aborts typically occur because of communication issues with an Image Processing and Storage Unit (IPSU). 

ACME’s Mass Flow Controllers (MFCs) used to control the flow of fuel and nitrogen have unfortunately not always performed adequately, where the flow rates have not 

85 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

always been appropriately steady.  The resulting effects on the flame behavior are tentatively assumed to be minor, but this still needs to be assessed. 

While it didn’t prevent the acquisition of scientific data, the intended igniter arm, which establishes the relative positioning of the igniter tip and burner, was not found for the round-2 hardware set-up.  Therefore, an alternate arm, which positioned the igniter further from the burner than intended, was used for the 3 Sept. 2020 testing. Fortunately, the flames were still reliably ignited and the proper igniter arm was found and installed prior to the subsequent test day. 

However, it was determined that the 3 Sept. 2020 science objectives were not accomplished because the fuel didn’t always transition to the intended test flow rate. As a result of concerns about the ignition success because of the use of the alternate igniter arm, the decision was made to repeatedly ignite the flame at the same fuel flow rate and then subsequently adjust it to the desired test flow rate.  The first attempt was successful, but the transition time was shortened to save fuel, etc. which unfortunately prevented the flow transition.  In other words, the transition time was too short where that wasn’t discovered until review of the data days later.  As a result, the tests were generally conducted at the same fuel flow rate, where that was meant to be a variable.  As a result, the data currently doesn’t allow a full assessment of the effect of the burner size on the flammability.  Therefore, a make-up test day in Increment 64 has been planned to address this shortcoming. 

##### **C. Completeness/Quality of Data** 

The number of flames ignited in Increments 58 and 59 significantly exceeds the planned tests – under the tested conditions (25 mm burner and ethylene as fuel) - which were specified as required in the ACME Integrated Science Requirements Document (ISRD).  A total of 25 such tests were required, while 62 flames with those conditions were ignited. As such, more than twice as many flames were ignited as had been planned. 

An additional 48 tests were specified as required in the ISRD to fully accomplish the BRE science objectives, i.e., where a total of 73 test points were required.  While there were issues with the Increment 63 testing, e.g., discussed in section B, 47 flames were ignited in Sept.-Oct. 2020, where 6 additional test days are currently planned for Increment 64 (including the make-up day previously discussed).  As such, it is expected that far more tests will be conducted than had been originally planned for BRE’s second round. 

It should be understood that a complete set of image data is not necessarily available for all tests.  Recorded image data is sometimes deleted without downlinking.  This is done because of operational constraints which limit the amount of data that can be managed per test day.  Given the constraint, the decision is sometimes made to sacrifice some image data (e.g., from the HiBMs camera) to allow additional tests to be conducted.  This is intentionally done to maximize the scientific return of each day of testing within the project constraints. 

86 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **_From BRE Co-I Peter Sunderland:_** 

The video and image data of the testing produced satisfactory data to display the nature of the burning sequence from ignition to self-extinction or fuel termination. However, the image quality is less than was hoped for.  The ACME ops camera provides video, but only offers 0.3 megapixels of resolution, and for many flames it is too zoomed out.  The GigE camera has poor sensitivity in the blue, and after the ignition transient all our flames have been blue.  This camera only offers 1.4 megapixels of resolution and requires about 1 s exposures for blue flames, which is far too slow to be considered video.  The images provided by both cameras are, according to UMD marketing staff, at too low resolution to be used in press releases. In round 2 we will increase the gain on the GigE camera, an option we were unaware of in round 1. 

Data quality is of course dependent on the experimental hardware.  But neglecting that limitation on data quality, the cameras are sensitive to radiation where pixels gradually become ‘hot’ over time.  While these bright spots are addressed in image post-processing, they are a degradation of the image quality. 

See the discussion in III.D regarding fouling or degradation of the paint on the BRE burner’s heat flux sensors. 

It is possible that the tests are subtly affected by the acceleration environment, but there is not obvious indication of this and it has thus far not been evaluated with available measurements from the Space Acceleration Measurement System (SAMS).  Note that although the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), in which the experiment is conducted, is equipped with a Passive Rack Isolation System (PaRIS) it was not utilized for BRE (or any ACME) testing because of the crew time needed to enable and disable the isolation before and after testing. 

Regardless of the shortcomings discussed above, a great wealth of experimental data has been secured and BRE’s first round is considered to be successful despite the frequent saturation of the radiometer data discussed in section III.B.2. 

##### **D. Status of Data Analysis (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student Parham Dehghani)** 

All measured transient raw data (e.g., heat flux sensors, thermocouples, radiometers, mass flow meters and chamber pressure sensor) have been converted to engineering units and plotted using the calibrations provided in ACME’s Software Interface Design Description (ACME-DOC-010 Rev. D). 

All of the round 1 tests have had the heat flux sensor data reduced using their original calibrations.  As presented below, the data is being processed to address the effect of the combustion product water condensation on the sensor surfaces. 

87 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

The primary measurements are used, by two techniques, to obtain the net heat flux from the flame to the burner surface. In the steady state, this net heat flux divided by the mass flow rate per unit area of the fuel mixture gives the heat of gasification of the emulated fuel.  The key hypothesis is that the heat of combustion, smoke point, and heat of gasification of the fuel mixture will represent a real solid fuel (or liquid fuel absorbed in an inert matrix).  Thus, achieved steady burning would represent the potential for real solid fuels to achieve steady burning in microgravity. Other than for a candle flame, this has not been shown before. 

The first technique uses two thermopile heat flux gages embedded in the burner surface. One gage is at the center the other is offset from the center ( _r_ = 9.5 mm). As the sensors are not cooled, but rise with temperature as the flame grows, water from its combustion products can condense and then evaporate from the sensor.  This occurs, as the dew point for combustion products is about 60-70<sup>o</sup> C, and the sensor begins at ambient temperature and can achieve temperatures over 100<sup>o</sup> C.   Thus, condensation can occur at temperatures below the dew point that leads to added heat to the sensor.  Above the dew point, the condensed water will evaporate and reduce the heat to the sensor.  When the sensor is dry and above 100<sup>o</sup> C, it will only measure the heat flux from the flame gases as intended. 

We have duplicated this condensation effect in 1-g laboratory experiments with the duplicative NASA BRE burner.  Fig. 1 illustrates the condensation and evaporation effect in the ISS tests.  The offset sensor in red shows a clear jump increase when the water fully evaporates.  More analysis is needed to properly correct for this effect.  Fortunately, by the end of the test shown here (and for all of the other data), this water effect is gone. 



**<mark>Figure 1.</mark>** <mark>Typical net heat flux of the heat flux sensors.</mark> 

88 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>The second effect on the heat flux sensors is that the heat may have fouled their paint. This could then affect their calibration.  Figure 2 shows an image of the ISS burner after round 1 tests were concluded.   It can be seen that the paint on the sensors is degraded. This was the original manufacturer’s paint, instead of that used for the copper top.  The copper paint appears fine. We worked with the heat flux gage manufacturer to explore the effect of heat damage on the calibration of the sensors. The results showed that the calibration was stable and didn’t change during the process of heating and cooling.</mark> 

**<mark>Figure 2.</mark>** <mark>Paint degradation on heat flux sensors.</mark> 

<mark>The second technique to determine the heat flux is the copper calorimeter forming the top of the burner.  The temperature rise of the copper is key to that determination. Figure 3 shows that typically the copper temperature is nearly uniform and smooth in time. It does not appear that condensation or evaporation is having any effect on its temperature behavior. So, we believe we are justified in using this measurement to predict the overall average heat flux from the flame to the burner.</mark> 

89 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

**Figure 3.** Copper and heat flux sensor temperatures in test 19070D1. 

<mark>The calorimeter required a new calibration to account for heat being absorbed through the bevel edge of copper plate.  So, control surfaces are now adjusted (i.e., the edge and burner back surface area, A</mark> back <mark>) and then optimized for new set of calibration constants which are specific to the burner used in ISS tests (i.e., mc</mark> Cu <mark>, h</mark> back <mark>, h</mark> rod <mark>, and e</mark> 0 <mark>).  The calorimeter represents the average heat flux to the surface and is being used to obtain heats of gasification.  Figure 4 shows the transient plots of test 19066D5. The test conditions for the plots used in this report are presented in Table 2. We found out that there is significant heat absorption by the bevel edge of the burner that induces error to the calorimeter heat flux. A theoretical analysis was implied to estimate the radiative, convective and re-radiation heat flux to and from the top surface and the bevel edge of the burner which helped in estimation of the error induced to the calorimeter data.</mark>
