ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

Our analysis of the radiometer sensor data has indicated its consistent ability to measure the flame overall radiation heat loss fraction.  The method to determine the radiative heat flux component to the burner is more complex, and it is based on the geometrical details of the radiometers and burner surface relative distance and orientation. Also, the burner surface will re-radiate heat that impinges the radiometer and it needs to be corrected. All of the available radiation data are processed for the first round of testing and an empirical radiation correlation with flame height is found. 

<mark>The video images of all the flames have been compiled and are being quantified in terms of flame shapes. Also, a summary booklet of all of the key findings along with heat flux and surface temperature data for each test was compiled.</mark> 

<mark>Alexander Snegirev, our Russian partner, has attempted a numerical simulation of at least one of the ISS BRE tests.  That work is still in progress.  It should be remarked his student won the Student Prize for Best Presentation at the 9</mark><sup>th</sup> <mark>International Seminar on Fire and Explosion Hazards held in St. Petersburg, Russia in April 2019 for simulating our 5-s BRE drop tests.</mark> 

##### **E. Preliminary Research Findings (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student Parham Dehghani)** 

<mark>The burner has shown that quasi-steady burning is possible for real solids with a flat surface of 25 mm diameter. Up to now most flammability studies in microgravity have focused on flame spread with a wind for concurrent spread and at least a self-induced wind for opposed flow spread. We already knew that this would be possible from normalgravity research. The burning of liquids, as in a wick of a candle flame and small droplets, has been shown to produce steady flames.  However, many droplet flames were found to extinguish owing to radiative cooling of the flame.  We see this as well in the extended ISS microgravity tests. However, this extinction was predominately seen for nominal ambient conditions of 21% oxygen and 14.7 psia. At conditions of 27% oxygen, 10.2 psia and 34% oxygen, 8.2 psia, the flames generally persisted for 3 minutes or more. These are all atmospheric conditions that NASA is considering for future spacecraft</mark> 

<mark>Figure 5 shows a typical test of ignition and flame growth. Although extinction occurred at 45 s with the flame still growing, the heat flux was nearly steady by 30 s. Therefore, a heat of gasification (</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>) could be estimated. This was done by using the arithmetic average of the center and offset heat flux sensor as the net average heat flux.</mark> 

92 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022

<mark>Figures 6 through 9 illustrate results for nominal atmospheric conditions of 26.5% oxygen and 0.7 atm (10.2 psia), and 34% oxygen and 0.56 atm (8.2 psia), respectively with two figures each.  Both use 50% mixtures of nitrogen and ethylene giving a fuel mixture heat of combustion of 24 kJ/g.  Both of these tests reached quasi-steady conditions and were terminated by shutting off the fuel.  The plots of transient heat flux shown by Figures 7 and 9, show the condensation effect removed after 80 s and 40 s respectively and arithmetic average of the two heat flux gauge measurements at the end of each test is recorded as steady surface heat flux which are used to obtain heats of gasification.</mark>

<mark>A simple analysis based on geometrical view factor of surface with respect to the flame sheet, allowed us to estimate the fraction of the total available heat that impinges surface. Adding up the convective, radiative and re-radiation from surface results in total average heat flux that gives theoretical heats of gasification when divided by fuel mixture mass flux.</mark>

<mark>The experimental flammability maps are shown in Figures 14 and 15.  Both plots indicate a decreasing, inverse asymptotic-like curve that approaches zero, for heat of gasification with mass flux.  It is surprising that the data cluster about a nearly singular trend. However, both plots show the heat of gasification is up to 20 % higher for the lower heat of combustion 23.6 kJ/g.  Our experiments in University of Maryland lab has shown, with the BRE in 1-g, that similar results for propane-nitrogen mixtures have the opposite behavior with heats of gasification decreasing with heat of combustion for the same mass flux.  But again, the data mostly cluster about one trend line except for very low heats of combustion.  However, the 1-g corresponding heat of combustion data shift far to the right and down from the microgravity data of Figures 14 and 15.  For example, the 1-g data would indicate ( 𝑚</mark> ̇ <mark>”, 𝐿 ) points of about 30 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, 2 kJ/g and 10, 0.5.  Moreover, our previous work in a 5- s drop facility suggested similar data trends but with an apparent vertical asymptote at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, rather than 1 as shown here.  Obviously, the current data at durations of minutes are more accurate than those limited to 5 s.  However, it has been shown that the ISS heat flux data even at self-extinction are theoretically nearly steady.  Figures 14 and 15 include the heats of gasification derived from the self-</mark> 

98 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>extinguished endpoint data as well.  Remarkably, the self-extinguished not-so-steady data follow the same trend as the steady data.</mark> 

<mark>Let us interpret Figures 14 and 15 in terms of the flammability of real materials in microgravity.  Typical melting plastics have heats of gasification ranging from about 2 to 3 kJ/g, with effective heats of gasification for charring plastics above 4 kJ/g.  Some selected literature values for materials with heats of combustion in the BRE range are listed in Table 3.  The accuracy of the calorimeter heat flux is found to be less than the two sensors, thus, we relied on the arithmetic average of the two heat flux sensors data for obtaining the flammability map.  However, same results are shown considering the calorimeter data.  Figure 14. would suggest that polyethylene (PE) and polypropylene (PP) would burn in microgravity at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.  Nylon and PMMA would burn at about 2.5 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, and the phenolic glass fiber composite would burn at about 1 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.</mark>

<mark>One important fire safety consideration, based on the findings of this study, is that solid materials with heats of combustion in the range 23.6 to 47.2 kJ/g and heats of gasification from about 1 to 10 kJ/g can burn in microgravity. The NASA alternative flammability Test 2 [1] using the Cone Calorimeter can measure these material properties. As this study develops a more complete understanding, NASA may wish to put more emphasis into Test 2.</mark>

#### **Appendix E. 30-Day Operational Accomplishments Report for ISS Increment 64** 

25 June 2021 

**30-day Operational Accomplishments Report … for the International Space Station (ISS) Increment 64** 

#### **ACME Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments BRE Burning Rate Emulator** 

_BRE is one of several independent experiments that are part of the ACME project, which uses a single modular set of hardware to carry out studies of flames burning gaseous fuels in the ISS’ Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR).  The investigation was carried out in two separate rounds, where the first and second rounds were respectively conducted during Increments 58-59 and 6364.  While this report was prepared for Increment 64, it comprehensively addresses the operations in all four increments._ 

Prepared by the PI team and Dennis Stocker (dennis.p.stocker@nasa.gov), the ACME Project Scientist, who is the primary contact for BRE and ACME information. 

Principal Investigator (PI): Prof. James G. Quintiere, University of Maryland, College Park, MD Co-Investigator (Co-I): Prof. Peter B. Sunderland, U. of Maryland, College Park, MD Co-Investigator (Co-I): Dr. John L. de Ris, FM Global (retired) Collaborator: Prof. Alexander Yu. Snegirev, Peter the Great St. Petersburg Polytechnic University, <mark>St. Petersburg, Russia</mark> Project Scientist (PS): Dennis P. Stocker, NASA Glenn Research Center, Cleveland, OH PS team member: Dr. Uday G. Hegde, Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH 

#### **I. Introduction** 

The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment is conducted in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station, as part of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project.  BRE is focused on spacecraft fire prevention.  More specifically, BRE’s goals are to improve our fundamental understanding of materials flammability, such as extinction behavior and the conditions needed for sustained combustion, and to assess the relevance of existing flammability test methods for low and partial-gravity environments.  A flat porous burner fed with gaseous fuel simulates the burning of solid and liquid fuels, where measurements are made of the thermal feedback (i.e., to the burner) upon which the vaporization of condensed-phase fuels is dependent.  A small number of gaseous fuels (including mixtures with inert gases) are used to simulate the burning of fuels such as paper, plastic, and 

108 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

alcohol by matching properties including the heats of combustion and gasification, the surface temperature, and smoke point.   The experiment’s results can establish fuel properties that directly relate to fire hazards in spacecraft, and if effective in microgravity could provide an efficient, safe, and comprehensive technique for assessing flammability and selecting fireresistant spacecraft materials. 

BRE’s specific experimental objectives are as follows, where it must be emphasized that NASA does not currently plan to carry out the desired tests for objective B. 

- J. Observe burning behavior in long-duration microgravity for CH4/N2 and C2H4/N2 mixtures on porous circular burners of 25 and 50 mm diameter.  Consider O2/N2 ambients with various pressures and ambient O2 concentrations and pressures.  NASAconsidered atmosphere for human space missions will be examined: 14.7 psia, 21 % oxygen; 10.2 psia, 26.5 %; and 8.2 psia, 34 % oxygen.  Emphasize fuel conditions that can be related to a diverse range of condensed fuels. 

- K. Consider the conditions of Objective 1 for ambients of O2/CO2 (desired). 

- L. Consider the conditions of Objectives 1 – 2 for extinction limits. 

The observations will enable quantification of the microgravity behavior of condensed fuels (i.e., liquids and non-charring plastics). 

Microgravity studies of flames allow for a range of simplifications and provide various advantages over normal-gravity studies.  For example, the flicker of flame, which is a buoyancy-driven (i.e., gravity-driven) instability, is eliminated yielding quasi-steady flames in microgravity.  Length scales are increased in microgravity flames facilitating analysis of the flame structure. Momentum-dominated flames, which are important for most practical combustion, can be studied at low velocities to simplify analysis.  Microgravity flames tend to have a much stronger sensitivity to their atmosphere and exhibit a much broader range of characteristics than normalgravity flames because of the near absence of buoyant entrainment.  The long residence times in microgravity flames can lead to strong soot production, but many microgravity flames are soot free.  The absence of buoyancy makes microgravity flames advantageous for studies of limit and stability behavior where chemical kinetics are important.  With these advantages, microgravity testing can lead to new understanding of the complex nature of combustion – regardless of the gravitational environment of the combustion application. 

#### **II. Methods/Research Operations** 

##### **A. Discussion of Method/Protocol** 

BRE and other ACME experiments are configured, but not conducted, by the ISS crew. The crew’s role is to set up the hardware, gas bottles, etc. for each set of tests.  The most common changes include the gas bottles on the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) and the mass flow controllers on the modular ACME chamber insert. 

109 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

All testing is commanded from the ground, specifically from the operations center at the NASA Glenn Research Center.  The science portion of the testing is typically conducted in a nominally automated mode using pre-programmed scripts.  However, it could alternately be conducted in a ‘manual’ mode, where changes are made to a control parameter in response to the analog video downlink and the numerical data available in the telemetry stream.  For example, gas flow rate(s) can be adjusted after quasi-steady data has been collected.  But testing has not been conducted in a ‘manual’ mode thus far for BRE or ACME in general. 

Testing is not conducted when thrusters fire or spacecraft dock or undock from ISS to ensure an acceptable acceleration environment. 

Data is downlinked between each set of tests, i.e., day of testing, to allow for preliminary analysis and requisite planning for subsequent testing. 

#### **III. Results** 

##### **A. Baselined Activities Completed – Pre-flight, In-flight, Post-Flight** 

The first round of BRE tests began in February 2019, during Increment 58, when a total of 45 flames were ignited.  That round was completed in April 2019, during Increment 59, when an additional 17 flames were ignited.  BRE’s second-round flame testing began in Sept. 2020, during Increment 63, in which 47 flames were ignited.  The second and final round of tests was completed in Jan. 2021, during Increment 64, when another 85 flames were ignited.  As a result, 62 and 132 flames were respectively ignited in the first and second rounds of testing for a total of 194 flames. 

The ‘interruption’ in the BRE testing was intentionally planned years in advance to enable the PI team to review the initial results in detail and with that knowledge plan the second round of tests to maximize its scientific outcome.  However, the break was longer than had been planned because of combination of hardware issues and limited crew availability.  As a result, BRE’s second round was rescheduled to occur after both rounds of the preceding ACME experiment, Structure and Response of Spherical Diffusion Flames (s-Flame), was completed, where that occurred in July 2020.  A factor in that decision was the delayed start of commercial crew launches and understanding that crew availability would be limited until such crew launches entered an operational, rather than demonstration, phase. 

All first-round tests were conducted with the 25-mm diameter BRE burner while burning ethylene (C2H4) - sometimes diluted with nitrogen.  The tests were carried out at a range of chamber pressures and oxygen concentrations, generally representative of possible crew environments in future spacecraft or habitats.  More specifically, testing was especially focused on atmospheric conditions at (1) 14.7 psia, 21% oxygen; (2) 10.2 psia, 26.5% oxygen; and (3) 8.2 psia, 34% oxygen.  In all cases, the atmosphere was diluted with 

110 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

nitrogen and any combustion products (e.g., water vapor and carbon dioxide) from preceding tests.  An exception is tests conducted after the chamber atmosphere was vented and refilled, such as the first test of the day.  But during tests days, the atmosphere was typically refreshed once or twice between sets of tests. 

Second round testing was carried out including both BRE burner diameters, i.e., 25 and 50 mm, and on some days with methane (CH4) – again sometimes diluted with nitrogen. Therefore, the second round of tests featured a broader range of conditions than investigated during the first round.  However, in contrast to the first round most of the round 2 tests were carried out at roughly 14.7 psia, although pressure has a secondary effect on flames.  Meanwhile, the tests in this round were carried out at various oxygen concentrations as in the first round but were more often conducted with ~40% oxygen, where oxygen of course has a strong effect on combustion. 

BRE does not have living subjects like a life science experiment. The tests are independently conducted with two different gaseous fuels, methane and ethylene.  Both are simple hydrocarbon gases for which the chemistry is well understood.  Methane (CH4) is the primary component of natural gas and tends to produce low levels of soot given the absence of carbon-carbon bonds.  Meanwhile, ethylene (C2H4), is normally highly sooting because of its carbon double bond.  Tests are conducted with pure fuel and fuel mixed with nitrogen, where the fuel and nitrogen were premixed on the ground prior to launch for some cases.  But the ACME hardware also allows for simultaneous flow of fuel and nitrogen to the burner, allowing for on-orbit fuel dilution and testing at a continuum of fuel concentrations. 

There are no samples (e.g., of soot) collected for BRE, where all ISS results are electronically transmitted to the ground.  That is furthermore the case for all currently planned ACME experiments. 

A summary log of the flames ignited for BRE during Increments 58 and 59 (the first round of tests) and Increment 63 and 64 (the second round) is found in Table 1.  The table includes a limited overview of the test conditions.  For completeness, a row was included for testing on 1 April 2019 (GMT 091) even though it was quickly discovered that the hotwire igniter tip had failed at the end of the preceding test day.  As a result, flames were not ignited until a few days later after astronaut Nick Hague had replaced the igniter tip. 

111

1 Feb. 2022

##### **B. List of Preflight, In-flight, Postflight Anomalies** 

###### **1. Mission anomalies** 

There were no significant anomalies hindering BRE operations during Increments 59 and 63, but there was one in Increment 58 during the preparations for BRE testing (that was mistakenly not addressed in the 30-day report including that increment).  It was discovered on Feb. 4 (GMT 035) that the pressure was low in a check gas bottle for the CIR’s Gas Chromatograph Instrumentation Package (GCIP).  The bottle had 

112 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

been on the space station for 9 years, and the low pressure was attributed to a nominal leak rate.  The check gas was for calibration of the GCIP measurements and not needed for regular operations which proceeded unhindered.  The bottle was replaced on June 13 to enable the necessary calibration. 

The anomaly in Increment 64 was similar in that there was a slow leak of the GCIP’s argon carrier gas.  As a result, the bottle valves for all of the instrument’s gas bottles were closed on Dec. 29, 2020 (GMT 364) making those measurements unavailable for the BRE’s last three test days. 

**2. Any other difficulties in obtaining scientific data** 

BRE’s primary problem was that the sensors for measuring the thermal radiation often saturated.  Although the measurements were not required to meet experiment success, they were considered sufficiently critical that BRE was equipped with its own radiometer array, featuring three thermopile detectors.  Estimates had been made of the signal levels based on 5-second drop tests since the hot gases tend to blanket the flame in the absence of buoyancy and thereby remain within the view of the radiometers.  As a result, the signal levels are strongly dependent on gravity.  The estimates were not part of the science requirements and may not have been sufficiently tracked in the hardware development.  Furthermore, the ISS tests lasted as long as 5 minutes, which is over four times the envisioned BRE test duration and 60 times the duration of the drop tests.  With the fixed amplification of the radiometer signals, the data was frequently lost - other than the knowledge that the limits were exceeded.  An engineering review failed to identify an acceptable solution and BRE testing continued unchanged during the first round, i.e., during increments 58 and 59. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

The radiometry array proved problematic as its sensors were mostly out of range and quickly became saturated.  However, for some of the small size flames, these data were complete.  Those data will be vital to assessing the role of flame and combustion product radiation. 

Saturation of the radiometers was partially mitigated in the round 2 testing, during increments 63 and 64, through use of ACME’s standard array instead of the one developed specifically for BRE. 

Ignition was not always successful.  As previously discussed, it was discovered on 1 April 2019 (GMT 091) that the hot-wire igniter tip had failed on the preceding test day, preventing further flame testing until it could be replaced.  While such circumstances are uncommon for ACME operations, BRE tests were sometimes conducted under conditions, e.g., for igniter activation and deactivation, which led to momentary flashes without sustained ignition.  Given that unplanned crew time is required to replace failed igniter tips, the BRE ops team limited - to varying degrees - the igniter voltage and the time that it was energized within the flame.  The latter was 

113 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

known from earlier research in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) to degrade the hot-wire igniter tip and hasten its demise.  It is notable that the flashes of flame (across the burner surface) sometimes continued briefly after the igniter was deactivated and retracted.  In one case on the first day of testing, there was excess fuel and a ‘fireball’ was ignited which promptly quenched.  Ignition also sometimes failed due to a software limitation where the fuelFlowIgnitionDeltaMs parameter which specifies the relative timing of the fuel flow and igniter activation must be evenly divisible by 100 (ms).  This limitation wasn’t initially understood and the igniter didn’t energize as a result. 

###### **_From the BRE PI Jim Quintiere:_** 

<mark>It was realized that the igniter for the burner gases could only be applied for up to 4 seconds, on insertion and retraction.  This produced an unanticipated problem, as the time for the burner gases to reach the igniter could be tens of seconds depending on the flow rate.  Also, after the first test, the burner would contain that fuel mixture mixed with the chamber gas.  Thus the burner would need to be flushed with nitrogen before the second fuel mixture was initiated.</mark> 

<mark>Intense communication with the NASA team proceeded the day before the first test. The gas volume of the burner along with the feed pipe volume was supplied by NASA.  Accordingly ground experiments at UMD were conducted with an identical NASA BRE burner along with its piping volume from the flow valve. These experiments and theory were used to develop an algorithm for determining the relative timing of the igniter and gas flow activation that will result in ignition.</mark> 

<mark>Day 1 [of the first round] was conducted with rough estimates made on the fly, and fortunately nearly all of the tests led to a satisfactory ignition.  By Day 2 an algorithm was established and successfully used for the remainder of the testing. This algorithm will need to be refined and applied for the larger 50 mm diameter burner that will be used in Round 2.</mark> 

<mark>A document on the development of the algorithm and its refinement for the larger burner [was] prepared and … shared with the NASA team to facilitate [ignition in] round 2.</mark> 

The second day of round-1 flame testing was delayed to allow for the development of a new ignition procedure, where concerns arose that the mixture of gas flowing from the burner wasn’t sufficiently well understood, i.e., in terms of concentrations.  This concern had to do with the burner’s large internal volume relative to that of the burners used for the other ACME experiments.  To be clear, this issue had to do with the analysis of results rather than the achievement of ignition.  Re-planning was also necessary early in BRE testing because the test durations were often much longer than had earlier been envisioned by the investigators.  While these factors didn’t directly interfere with the acquisition of test data, they did slow progress. 

114 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

While it rarely occurred, BRE tests sometimes automatically aborted (due to the controlling software script), where that is typically prior to gas flow and the activation of the igniter.  As a result, there is little lost other than the time associated with the failed test attempt.   Such aborts typically occur because of communication issues with an Image Processing and Storage Unit (IPSU). 

ACME’s Mass Flow Controllers (MFCs) used to control the flow of fuel and nitrogen have unfortunately not always performed adequately, where the flow rates have not always been appropriately steady.  The resulting effects on the flame behavior are tentatively assumed to be minor, but this still needs to be assessed. 

While it didn’t prevent the acquisition of scientific data, the intended igniter arm, which establishes the relative positioning of the igniter tip and burner, was not found for the round-2 hardware set-up.  Therefore, an alternate arm, which positioned the igniter further from the burner than intended, was used for the 3 Sept. 2020 testing. Fortunately, the flames were still reliably ignited and the proper igniter arm was found and installed prior to the subsequent test day. 

However, it was determined that the 3 Sept. 2020 science objectives were not accomplished because the fuel didn’t always transition to the intended test flow rate. As a result of concerns about the ignition success because of the use of the alternate igniter arm, the decision was made to repeatedly ignite the flame at the same fuel flow rate and then subsequently adjust it to the desired test flow rate.  The first attempt was successful, but the transition time was shortened to save fuel, etc. which unfortunately prevented the flow transition.  In other words, the transition time was too short where that wasn’t discovered until review of the data days later.  As a result, the tests were generally conducted at the same fuel flow rate, where that was meant to be a variable.  As a result, the data currently doesn’t allow a full assessment of the effect of the burner size on the flammability.  Therefore, a make-up test day in Increment 64 has been planned to address this shortcoming. 

During testing on 23 Dec. 2020, it was discovered that the lowest of the 14-micron silicon carbide fibers on the Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP) fiber array had broken. After some discussion, testing continued that day.  Given that the translating array had five ceramic fibers, the loss of one was not a significant science loss.  But it was a concern for crew safety.  Recovery plans were developed and enacted on January 18 to remove the broken fragments from the harp-like array.  Unfortunately, the next fiber was broken during the delicate operations where the fibers are only spaced 5 mm apart.  As a result, the last two BRE test days were conducted with only three rather than five fibers in the TFP array.  ACME does have alternate arrays, but also has months of remaining testing, so the decision was made to continue testing with only 3 fibers. 

115 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

##### **C. Completeness/Quality of Data** 

The 194 flames ignited for BRE significantly exceeds the 73 tests which were specified as required in the ACME Integrated Science Requirements Document (ISRD).  As such, more than 2.5 times as many flames were ignited than had been planned. 

It should be understood that a complete set of image data is not necessarily available for all tests.  Recorded image data is sometimes deleted without downlinking.  This is done because of operational constraints which limit the amount of data that can be managed per test day.  Given the constraint, the decision is sometimes made to sacrifice some image data (e.g., from the HiBMs camera) to allow additional tests to be conducted.  This is intentionally done to maximize the scientific return of each day of testing within the project constraints. 

###### **_From BRE Co-I Peter Sunderland:_** 

The video and image data of the testing produced satisfactory data to display the nature of the burning sequence from ignition to self-extinction or fuel termination. However, the image quality is less than was hoped for.  The ACME ops camera provides video, but only offers 0.3 megapixels of resolution, and for many flames it is too zoomed out.  [As a clarification, the ops camera has a fixed lens and its primary purpose is not flame measurements.]  The GigE camera has poor sensitivity in the blue, and after the ignition transient all our flames have been blue.  This camera only offers 1.4 megapixels of resolution and requires about 1 s exposures for blue flames, which is far too slow to be considered video.  The images provided by both cameras are, according to UMD marketing staff, at too low resolution to be used in press releases.  In round 2 we will increase the gain on the GigE camera, an option we were unaware of in round 1. 

Data quality is of course dependent on the experimental hardware.  But neglecting that limitation on data quality, the cameras are sensitive to radiation where pixels gradually become ‘hot’ over time.  While these bright spots are addressed in image post-processing, they are a degradation of the image quality. 

See the discussion in III.D regarding fouling or degradation of the paint on the BRE burner’s heat flux sensors. 

It is possible that the tests are subtly affected by the acceleration environment, but there is not obvious indication of such effects and it has thus far not been evaluated with available measurements from the Space Acceleration Measurement System (SAMS). Note that although the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), in which the experiment is conducted, is equipped with a Passive Rack Isolation System (PaRIS) it was not utilized for BRE (or any ACME) testing because of the crew time needed to enable and disable the isolation before and after testing. 

116 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

Regardless of the shortcomings discussed above, a great wealth of experimental data has been secured and BRE’s first round is considered to be successful despite the frequent saturation of the radiometer data discussed in section III.B.2.  Meanwhile, the second round is tentatively considered to be highly successful, where final assessment will depend on further review and analysis of the data. 

- **D. Status of Data Analysis (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student** 

   - **Parham Dehghani; lightly edited by Dennis Stocker)** 

All measured transient raw data (e.g., heat flux sensors, thermocouples, radiometers, mass flow meters and chamber pressure sensor) have been converted to engineering units and plotted using the calibrations provided in ACME’s Software Interface Design Description (ACME-DOC-010 Rev. D). 

All of the round 1 and 2 tests have had the heat flux sensor data reduced using their original calibrations.  As presented below, the data is being processed to address the effect of the combustion product water condensation on the sensor surfaces. 

The primary measurements are used, by two techniques, to obtain the net heat flux from the flame to the burner surface. In the steady state, this net heat flux divided by the mass flow rate per unit area of the fuel mixture gives the heat of gasification of the emulated fuel.  The key hypothesis is that the heat of combustion, smoke point, and heat of gasification of the fuel mixture will represent a real solid fuel (or liquid fuel absorbed in an inert matrix).  Thus, achieved steady burning would represent the potential for real solid fuels to achieve steady burning in microgravity.  Other than for a candle flame, this has not been shown before. 

The first technique uses two thermopile heat flux gages embedded in the burner surface. One gage is at the center the other is offset from the center ( _r_ = 9.5 mm). As the sensors are not cooled, but rise with temperature as the flame grows, water from its combustion products can condense and then evaporate from the sensor.  This occurs, as the dew point for combustion products is about 60-70<sup>o</sup> C, and the sensor begins at ambient temperature and can achieve temperatures over 100<sup>o</sup> C.  Thus, condensation can occur at temperatures below the dew point that lead to added heat to the sensor.  Above the dew point, the condensed water will evaporate and reduce the heat to the sensor.  When the sensor is dry and above 100<sup>o</sup> C, it will only measure the heat flux from the flame gases as intended. 

We have duplicated this condensation effect in 1-g laboratory experiments with the duplicative NASA BRE burner.  Fig. 1 illustrates the condensation and evaporation effect in the ISS tests.  The offset sensor in red shows a clear jump increase when the water fully evaporates.  More analysis is needed to properly correct for this effect.  Fortunately, by the end of the test shown here (and for all of the other data), this water effect is gone. 

117 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

**<mark>Figure 1.</mark>** <mark>Typical net heat flux of the heat flux sensors.</mark> 

<mark>The second effect on the heat flux sensors is that the heat may have fouled their paint. This could then affect their calibration.  Figure 2 shows an image of the ISS burner after round 1 test were concluded.   It can be seen that the paint on the sensors is degraded. This was the original manufacturer’s paint, instead of that used for the copper top.  The copper paint appears fine. We worked with the heat flux gage manufacturer to explore the effect of heat damage on the calibration of the sensors. The results showed that the calibration was stable and didn’t change during the process of heating and cooling.</mark> 

**<mark>Figure 2.</mark>** <mark>Paint degradation on heat flux sensors.</mark> 

<mark>The second technique to determine the heat flux is the copper calorimeter forming the top of the burner.  The temperature rise of the copper is key to that determination.</mark>

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>Figure 3 shows that typically the copper temperature is nearly uniform and smooth in time. It does not appear that condensation or evaporation is having any effect on its temperature behavior. So, we believe we are justified in using this measurement to predict the overall average heat flux from the flame to the burner.</mark> 



**Figure 3.** Copper and heat flux sensor temperatures in test 19070D1. 

Recently it has come to our attention that the actual thermocouple and wiring types were as summarized in Table 2, below.  We used type K conversion coefficients for south (i.e., center copper) and far south (i.e., offset copper) thermocouples and type T for heat flux transducers respectively from NIST data set provided in this link: 

<u>https://srdata.nist.gov/its90/download/allcoeff.tab</u> 

**Table 2.** Thermocouple and wiring types for BRE burner. 



One sees that only the south (i.e., center copper) thermocouple has consistent type K wiring throughout.  This latter thermocouple recorded an initial temperature of 28 deg. Celsius from which we can infer that initial ambient temperature must be 28 deg. C applicable to all thermocouples. 

Table 2 shows the center transducer thermocouple was mismatched with a type B extension wire, and the offset transducer was mismatched with a type K extension wire. 

119 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

Meanwhile the far south (i.e., offset) copper plate thermocouple was of type K, but was mismatched with type B extension wiring. 

Ambient temperature before ignition is applicable to all thermocouples that can be obtained by using the igniter side and relay side thermocouples. This thermocouple and its extension wiring are of type K.  The BRE burner thermocouples output before test starts must match igniter side’s temperature. 

According to the _Intermediate Metal Law_ the extension wires do not affect the voltages since they are located at a constant temperature throughout their length.  Therefore, each thermocouple voltage is converted to temperature based on their thermocouple type (shown in Table 2). 

Since the south thermocouple output is the only one that shows higher temperature compared to other three and the igniter side temperature, it is concluded that this thermocouple is receiving a constant extra voltage of 0.127 mV.  Therefore, this spurious voltage should be subtracted from the south thermocouple voltage.  The resulting temperatures are shown in Figure 4. 



**Figure 4. Temperature measurements after modifications made for the heat flux transducers and far south thermocouple.** 

<mark>The calorimeter required a new calibration to account for heat being absorbed through the bevel edge of copper plate.  So, control surfaces are now adjusted (i.e., the edge and</mark>

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022)

<mark>burner back surface area, A</mark> back <mark>) and then optimized for new set of calibration constants which are specific to the burner used in ISS tests (i.e., mc</mark> Cu <mark>, h</mark> back <mark>, h</mark> rod <mark>, and e</mark> 0 <mark>).  The calorimeter represents the average heat flux to the surface and is being used to obtain heats of gasification.  Figure 5 shows the transient plots of test 19066D5. The test conditions for the plots used in this report are presented in Table 3. We found out that there is significant heat absorption by the bevel edge of the burner that induces error in the calorimeter heat flux. A theoretical analysis was employed to estimate the radiative, convective and re-radiation heat flux to and from the top surface and the bevel edge of the burner which helped in estimating the error induced in the calorimeter data.</mark>

Our analysis of the radiometer sensor data has indicated its consistent ability to measure the flame overall radiation heat loss fraction.  The method to determine the radiative heat flux component to the burner is more complex, and it is based on the geometrical details of the radiometers and burner surface relative distance and orientation.  Also, the burner surface will re-radiate heat that impinges the radiometer and it needs to be corrected. All of the available radiation data are processed for the first round of testing and an empirical radiation correlation with flame height was found.

##### **E. Preliminary Research Findings (submitted by BRE PI Jim Quintiere and doctoral student Parham Dehghani; lightly edited by Dennis Stocker)** 

<mark>The burner has shown that quasi-steady burning is possible for real solids with a flat surface of 25 mm diameter.  Up to now most flammability studies in microgravity have focused on flame spread with a wind for concurrent spread and at least a self-induced wind for opposed flow spread.  We already knew that this would be possible from normalgravity research.  The burning of liquids, as in a wick of a candle flame and small droplets, has been shown to produce steady flames.  However, many droplet flames were found to extinguish owing to radiative cooling of the flame.  We see this as well in the extended ISS microgravity tests. However, this extinction was predominately seen for nominal ambient conditions of 21% oxygen and 14.7 psia. At conditions of 27% oxygen, 10.2 psia and 34% oxygen, 8.2 psia, the flames generally persisted for 3 minutes or more. These are all atmospheric conditions that NASA is considering for future spacecraft</mark> 

<mark>Figure 6 shows a typical test of ignition and flame growth. Although extinction occurred at 45 s with the flame still growing, the heat flux was nearly steady by 30 s. Therefore, a heat of gasification (</mark> _<mark>L</mark>_ <mark>) could be estimated. This was done by using the arithmetic average of the center and offset heat flux sensor as the net average heat flux.</mark> 

122 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 



**Figure 6.** Flame growth from ignition to self-extinction in test 19056F1. The conditions are ethylene/N2,  _hc_ = 24 kJ/g, 4.55 g/m<sup>2</sup> s, 27% O2, 10.2 psia, _L_ = 0.66 kJ/g, extinction at 45 s. 

<mark>Figures 7 through 10 illustrate results for nominal atmospheric conditions of 26.5% oxygen and 0.7 atm (10.2 psia), and 34% oxygen and 0.56 atm (8.2 psia), respectively with two figures each.  Both use 50% mixtures of nitrogen and ethylene giving a fuel mixture heat of combustion of 24 kJ/g.  Both of these tests reached quasi-steady conditions and were terminated by shutting off the fuel.  The plots of transient heat flux shown by Figures 8 and 10, show the condensation effect removed after 80 s and 40 s respectively and arithmetic average of the two heat flux gauge measurements at the end of each test is recorded as steady surface heat flux which are used to obtain heats of gasification.</mark>

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022)

<mark>The experimental flammability maps are shown in Figures 15 and 16.  Both plots indicate a decreasing, inverse asymptotic-like curve that approaches zero, for heat of gasification with mass flux.  It is surprising that the data cluster about a nearly singular trend. However, both plots show that the heat of gasification is up to 20 % higher for the lower heat of combustion 23.6 kJ/g.  Our tests in the University of Maryland lab have shown, with the BRE in 1-g that similar results for propane-nitrogen mixtures have the opposite behavior with heats of gasification decreasing with heat of combustion for the same mass flux.  But again, the data mostly cluster about one trend line except for very low heats of combustion.  However, the corresponding 1-g heat of combustion data shift far to the right and down from the microgravity data of Figures 15 and 16.  For example, the 1-g data would indicate ( 𝑚</mark> ̇ <mark>”, 𝐿 ) points of about 30 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, 2 kJ/g and 10, 0.5.  Moreover, our previous work in a 5- s drop facility suggested similar data trends but with an apparent vertical asymptote at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, rather than 1 as shown here.  Obviously, the current data at durations of minutes are more accurate than those limited to 5 s.  However, it has been shown that the ISS heat flux data even at self-extinction are theoretically nearly steady.  Figures 15 and 16 include the heats of gasification derived from the selfextinguished endpoint data as well.  Remarkably, the self-extinguished not-so-steady data follow the same trend as the steady data.</mark> 

<mark>Let us interpret Figures 15 and 16 in terms of the flammability of real materials in microgravity.  Typical melting plastics have heats of gasification ranging from about 2 to 3 kJ/g, with effective heats of gasification for charring plastics above 4 kJ/g.  Some selected literature values for materials with heats of combustion in the BRE range are listed in Table 4.  The accuracy of the calorimeter heat flux is found to be less than the two sensors, thus, we relied on the arithmetic average of the two heat flux sensors data for obtaining the flammability map.  However, same results are shown considering the calorimeter data.  Figure 15 would suggest that polyethylene (PE) and polypropylene (PP) would burn in microgravity at about 3 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.  Nylon and PMMA would burn at about 2.5 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s, and the phenolic glass fiber composite would burn at about 1 g/m</mark><sup>2</sup> <mark>s.</mark> 

**Table 4.** Materials close to BRE heats of combustion and 

corresponding heats of gasification 

|Material|_LHV_, kJ/g|_L_, kJ/g|
|---|---|---|
|PE|43.4|1.9|
|PP|44|2.0|
|Nylon|28.8|1.5, 2.4|
|PMMA|24.2|1.6, 2.3|
|Phenolic glass fibers|22.0|7.3|



<mark>Round 2 tests were finished with 196 successful ignitions.  The measured heat flux by the transducers were used and the end point data of the long-duration tests (i.e., the tests in which the fuel flow was terminated as opposed to the tests that self-extinguished) were used to obtain heats of gasification.  The heats of gasification of these data from round 2 testing as a function of fuel mixture mass flux are shown in Fig. 19.  One important fire</mark> 

127 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

<mark>safety consideration, based on the findings of this study, is that solid materials with heats of combustion in the range 23.6 to 47.2 kJ/g and heats of gasification from about 1 to 10 kJ/g can burn in microgravity.  The NASA alternative flammability Test 2 [1] using the Cone Calorimeter can measure these material properties. As this study develops a more complete understanding, NASA may wish to put more emphasis into Test 2.</mark> 

<mark>Moreover, the flammability map based on our theoretical analysis is shown in Figure 17. It shows similar asymptotic trend and clearly indicates lower pressure and higher oxygen concentration as favorable conditions for long duration burning.</mark>

#### **<mark>Appendix H. ISS Operations per the Payload Weekly Science Summary</mark>** 

_This section is from Dennis Stocker’s log of ACME operations._ 

**BRE round 1, featuring tests with ethylene and the 25-mm burner** 

### **<mark>2019</mark>** 

##### **Week 16: January 11-17** 

David: On GMT 014, thank you for replacing the imaging modules in the CIR LLL-UV Imaging Package. Then on GMT 018, great job configuring the back of the CIR Optics Bench to support future ACME experiments. We appreciate all your hard work and diligence during the many intricate tasks! 

##### **Week 18: January 25-31** 

David: On GMT 031 & 032, great job configuring the ACME Chamber Insert to support the next ACME experiment, BRE (Burning Rate Emulator). We appreciate your attention to detail during the many complicated tasks! We especially appreciate you taking all the closeout photos of the newly installed hardware. We will check out and calibrate the newly installed hardware next week and perform the first test points late next week. 

##### **Week 19: February 1-7** 

Oleg & Anne: Great job replacing the CIR Manifold & GC Bottles this week! We these new bottles installed, we look forward to starting the next ACME experiment, BRE (Burning Rate Emulator). 

##### **Week 20: February 8-14** 

On GMT 039, we performed our first ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points. Unlike the other ACME experiments, BRE is focused on improving spacecraft fire safety rather than practical terrestrial combustion. More specifically, BRE uses gaseous fuels to determine the flammability of liquids and solids in microgravity by selecting test conditions to match key parameters. We performed nine successful tests burning ethylene on the 25-mm diameter burner, where the burner’s embedded sensors measure the heat flux that would normally vaporize a liquid or solid fuel. The tests were conducted under a range of oxygen concentrations and pressures, including some atmospheres that could be used for future crew vehicles or habitats. 



Figure 1. Sequential Monochrome Images of the First BRE Flame Stabilized on the Cylindrical Burner 

Figure 1 shows a sequence of three images from BRE’s first on-orbit test. The first image shows that the flame grew to become nominally hemispherical (over the burner outlet, which can also be seen). The second image shows a hole in the top of the flame due to radiative heat loss. However, the third image 

141 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

shows that the flame recovered, where the hole closed. In this latter phase of the test, measurements of chemiluminescence (from OH*) clearly reveal that the flame was oscillating at approximately 1 Hz. These oscillations (the opening and closing of the flame’s top) were seen to grow in magnitude and are associated with the flame’s approach to extinction. In the first test, this lasted for an extended time, but in other tests at lower oxygen concentrations the oscillations sometimes lasted 10 seconds or less before the flame quenched. BRE seeks to understand the flame extinction and the conditions under which the flames become unsteady and finally extinguish. 

##### **Week 21: February 15-21** 

Testing for the Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) experiment resumed on GMT 051 following a revision of the ignition procedure.  Eight successful tests were conducted burning pure ethylene, which is naturally a sooty fuel because of its double bond, but the soot produced during ignition quickly vanished leaving a pale blue flame, where the flame temperatures were insufficient to generate soot.  These flames grow to be nominally hemispherical, then become quasi-steady although weakening, and finally become unsteady and extinguish when radiative heat losses become too great.  The experiment’s goal is to improve our understanding of material flammability in microgravity and thereby guide the selection of materials use in spacecraft. 

###### **CAPTION TO VIDEO,** **_WHICH IS NOT INCLUDED IN THIS LOG_** 

The video from test 19039G1 is typical of the BRE tests conducted thus far, where the flame grows to become nominally hemispherical, during which time the soot produced at ignition vanishes. The flame then becomes quasi-steady, e.g., in terms of the flame shape and size, although it can be seen that the flame weakens with time – becoming dimmer and more diffuse.  Oxygen transport to the flame is inhibited because it is blanketed by an ever-thickening cloud of carbon dioxide and water vapor.  Eventually, radiative heat loss leads the top of the flame (on the video’s left) to locally extinguish, leaving a hole in the flame.  But the fuel and oxygen, which are normally separated by the flame, mix and the flame quickly reestablishes, filling the hole.  A pattern of oscillation begins, which also features periodic lifting of the flame base from the cylindrical burner (on the right of the video).  The oscillations grow in strength, where the flame becomes momentarily just a ring.  At the end, before complete extinction, the flame also partially detaches from the burner, leaving a crack in the flame’s side extending from the base to its top. 

The durations of at least the quasi-steady and unsteady periods are strongly dependent on the atmosphere in the chamber, where both the oxygen concentration and pressure have been varied.  As one might expect, longer durations are observed at higher oxygen concentrations. 

_Note that the brighter circle that can be seen in upper middle of the video is due to reflection from an optical sensor, which is not seen in the other three camera views._ 

##### **Week 22: February 22-28** 

On GMT 056, we performed ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points with a 50% ethylene/50% nitrogen mixture. The flames with 50% ethylene may be steadier than the 100% ethylene flames, which require more oxygen at the same total flow rate. The 50% ethylene flames still exhibited flame growth, a quasi-steady period, and oscillations leading to extinction. The flames also quickly became soot free (i.e., all blue flame) after ignition. 

142 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 



Ignition, extinction, and the three main phases of the BRE flame’s life can be seen in this example plot of the OH* chemiluminescence measured by one of the three PhotoMultipiler Tube (PMT) modules on the ACME Chamber Insert. OH* is an excited molecule that loses energy by emitting a photon of light at a specific wavelength (in the ultraviolet). In combustion of most fuels, there is oxygen, hydrogen, and lots of energy making OH* measurements a good indicator of the flame. 

This data shows that the flame is weakening during the quasi-steady period when the flame size and shape is nominally constant. The OH* measurement also clearly shows the oscillations that precede extinction and their growing strength. The flame’s oscillations (e.g., with the opening and closing of the top) are at a nominally constant frequency that is a little less than 1 Hz. The huge OH* oscillations are the flame’s death throes when it splits open from bottom to top and partially detaches from the burner. 

##### **Week 23: March 1-7** 

On GMT 064, Oleg Kononenko replaced the CIR Manifold #2 Bottle that was consumed during test points on GMT 063. For these ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points, we used higher fuel flow rates with the same fuel (50% ethylene/50% nitrogen) as the test points on GMT 056. We performed a total of six tests, where the first—in an atmosphere of 40% oxygen—was notable in that the flame didn’t extinguish during the five-minute test even though it was oscillating in the last three minutes. As expected, the flames in subsequent tests with higher flow rates grew to be significantly larger in size, and with their higher oxygen consumption, they extinguished in significantly shorter times. We observed dramatic oscillations with the large flames. Furthermore, these large oscillating flames became asymmetric; whereas, smaller BRE flames have been largely axisymmetric even when oscillating. 

143 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **CAPTION TO VIDEO,** **_WHICH IS NOT INCLUDED IN THIS LOG_** 

The test shown in this video is the first ACME test—of all the experiments thus far—to have such a high fuel flow rate. Initially, the flame behavior is similar to previous BRE tests. The flame is very sooty at ignition, due to ethylene’s double bond, where you can see the glowing soot particles pass through the flame. But the soot quickly fades away leaving a dim blue flame, where blue is the natural color of hydrocarbon flames. The flame expands to reach enough oxygen to consume the fuel. Meanwhile, the flame is blanketed by a thickening cloud of carbon dioxide and water vapor inhibiting the oxygen’s diffusion to the flame. With the poor oxygen transport, the flame weakens, and radiative heat loss leaves a hole in the flame. But oxygen then mixes with the fuel, causing the flame to quickly reestablish and the hole closes. 

With this flame’s large size, the edge of the flame opening can be seen to curve upward and outward—somewhat like a vase—where that is the fuel-lean rim of the flame edge. The opening and closing grows in strength as in previous tests, but the flame size is apparently so large that asymmetries begin. The flame eventually becomes mostly quenched with just a ring at the base and a highly dynamic spiral extending upward. As in previous tests, the flame partially detaches from the burner (right side of video), but in this case, the flame surprisingly persists for roughly 20 seconds despite the large-scale detachment. While careful analysis will be challenging, this flame is visually dramatic with its swirling pulsations and powerful death throes. 

##### **Week 24/01: March 8-14** 

On GMT 072, Oleg Kononenko replaced the CIR Manifold #4 Bottle to support upcoming ACME BRE test points with pure ethylene fuel, and he installed the CIR Manifold #1 Bottle to support the upcoming CIR FOMA Calibration. 

On GMT 066 & 070, we performed the remaining ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points using diluted ethylene fuel (50% ethylene/50% nitrogen). These tests were conducted with low fuel flow rates under atmospheric conditions that could be used for future crew exploration vehicles (or habitats), i.e., with elevated oxygen concentrations (between 26% and 36%) and reduced pressures. Compared to prior tests, these flames were steady and didn’t extinguish during the allotted test durations. 

Even though these flames appeared steady, measurements of OH* and CH* chemiluminescence revealed that they were slowly weakening as each test progressed. Chemiluminescence occurs when excited molecules at a high energy state drop to a lower energy state by emitting a photon of a certain wavelength. CH* chemiluminescence is why hydrocarbon flames are blue; meanwhile the OH* chemiluminescence is in the ultraviolet. 

Although the flames were weakening, the thermal radiation from each flame slowly increased because of the lack of buoyancy, which, in gravity, removes the hot combustion gases from the vicinity of a flame. Instead, these flames became blanketed by a thickening cloud of hot and radiating combustion products, namely carbon dioxide and water vapor. This accumulation of products inhibited the transport of oxygen to the flame. Yet, with the low flow of diluted fuel, the oxygen diffusion was enough to maintain these flames during each test’s duration. If the test durations weren’t limited, these flames would have eventually gone out, but it could have taken many minutes for that to happen.

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 



This plot illustrates the challenge faced in igniting some BRE tests. The plot is of OH* chemiluminescence, which is measured by a Photomultiplier Tube (PMT) during the ignition period. Chemiluminescence occurs when a molecule drops from a higher to a lower energy level by emitting a photon of a specific wavelength. In this case, the excited OH* emits light in ultraviolet (at 310 nm). While this light is not visible, it is a good indicator of the flame’s strength or intensity. This plot shows how the flame repeatedly flashed across the burner. The flame didn’t stabilize until after the igniter was retracted. Unfortunately, by that time the igniter tip had already burned out.

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 



Figure 1. Sample preliminary images from a BRE test, where both were processed from the same raw image file. Image (a) reveals the relative positions of the glowing ceramic fibers, while (b) is adjusted for determination of the temperature along the fibers. 

Figure 1 displays sample images, in which Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP) was used to allow optical determination of the temperature along the ceramic fibers where they are hot enough to visibly glow. The harp-like array of 14-micron SiC fibers was translated throughout the flame allowing measurement of the entire temperature field. The temperature is determined by comparison of the red, blue, and green color planes of the images, which were taken with a digital camera characterized for quantitative measurements. The color of the fiber’s glow is indicative of the temperature, where yellow is hotter than orange, which in turn is hotter than red. (The blue in the flame results from the flame chemistry rather than the fibers’ broadband thermal radiation.)

147 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

##### **Week 22: September 4-10** 

On GMT 247, we began the second round of testing for ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) with 13 flames ignited using ethylene fuel. While the first round of testing only used the 25-mm diameter burner, this round of testing began with the 50-mm diameter burner to better understand the effect of burner size on flame behavior. Even though these flames burned for a long time, they were unsteady and extremely dynamic. Since BRE’s research focus is on quasi-steady flame behavior, the remaining tests will use the 25-mm burner. 

In many of these 50-mm burner tests, heat loss caused the top of the flame to locally quench—creating a hole that allowed for increased mixing of the fuel and oxygen. The flame was able to reestablish, closing the hole in its top. These oscillations occurred repeatedly and grew in strength such that the flame became a ring instead of a dome. Later in the burn, the flame often partially detached from the burner, creating a hole in the side of the flame that would also open and close repeatedly. In some cases, this behavior transitioned to a more constant hole, where the partial flame would swirl around the burner’s rim as if it were “chasing its tail.” 

###### **CAPTION TO VIDEO,** **_WHICH IS NOT INCLUDED IN THIS LOG_** 

This clip from a test point video shows the flame dynamics observed in the latter portion of the test. This test is burning 100% ethylene fuel in a combustion chamber environment of about 32% oxygen/68% nitrogen. The flame, which in microgravity would normally be dome-shaped, has largely quenched where only a portion is burning. In this phase of the burn, the flame appears to have mostly detached from the burner. The flame swirls around the burner’s rim, where the leading edge is bright because of the fuel/oxidizer mixing that occurs where the flame has quenched. While the nature of the oscillations changed over time, they made up about 70% of the flame’s life. In fact, this flame was still oscillating when the fuel flow was shut off (at a preset time), and the flame went out. 

##### **Week 23: September 11 - 17** 

In support of ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points, Chris Cassidy replaced the burner, igniter arm and tip, and two mass flow controllers on the ACME Chamber Insert on GMT 258. This burner allows for steadier flames, and the mass flow controllers allow for slower nitrogen and fuel flow rates. 

##### **Week 24: September 18 - 24** 

On GMT 261, 13 ethylene flames were ignited for ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) with its primary burner, which has a 25-mm diameter outlet. Today’s flames were steadier than those with the 50-mm burner used on the previous test day. In fact, one of today’s tests appears to be the most stable BRE flame thus far, including the 62 BRE flames ignited in early 2019. The stability of flames in a no-breeze environment has important fire safety implications. The current approach to spacecraft fire is to stop ventilation so that the flames will quench, but the BRE results indicate that there are conditions under which flames will continue to burn in still air. 

On GMT 266, Anatoly Ivanishin replaced the bottles at CIR Manifold #2 and CIR Manifold #4. An issue with the CIR Manifold #4 bottle valve delayed testing this week. From crew photos and video, we determined that the valve is already fully open, which we confirmed with powered ops on GMT 267. The CIR Bottle Valve Cover was cross-threaded when last installed, which caused it to bind on the valve. When rotating the valve cover to remove it, the valve also rotated. We will be able to resume test points next week!

###### **ACME BRE Test Point Image** 



This image of a dome-shaped flame is from late in a test in which the blue flame remained quasi-steady (i.e., changes are occurring at such a slow rate that the flame can be considered constant). The burner is illuminated from the left in red. The test was conducted in an atmosphere of about 30% oxygen (with a balance of nitrogen and gases resulting from preceding tests) with a reduced pressure of roughly 0.6 atm. Future crew exploration may feature vehicles and habitats at elevated oxygen concentrations and reduced pressures to facilitate ExtraVehicular Activity (EVA). BRE includes testing under such conditions because of the increased fire hazard with elevated oxygen concentrations. 

##### **Week 25: September 25 - October 1** 

In support of ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points, Anatoly Ivanishin replaced the CIR GC Argon Bottle and opened the bottle valve at CIR Manifold #2 on GMT 272. He also took some very nice, detailed photos of the damaged CIR Bottle Valve Cover. 

##### **Week 26: October 2 - 8** 

On GMT 279, a record 21 flames were successfully ignited for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment using methane as the fuel, which was a first for this investigation. Methane is the primary component of natural gas and is a good contrast to ethylene, the fuel used in BRE’s preceding test days. Ethylene (C2H4) is naturally sooty in normal-gravity flames because of its C=C double bond; whereas, methane (CH4) has no carbon bonds. With a goal of improved spacecraft fire safety, BRE simulates the combustion of solid and liquid materials by matching key parameters in tests conducted with gaseous fuels. Testing with different gaseous fuels provides an understanding of flammability for a broader range of materials. 

149 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **ACME BRE Test Point Image** 



This preliminary false-colored image of a dome-shaped flame is from late in a test in which the blue flame remained quasi-steady through most of the test duration. Quasi-steady describes when changes are occurring at such a slow rate that the flame can be considered constant. This photo was taken shortly before the flame oscillated for a few seconds and then self-extinguished. While the flame’s top often locally quenches due to radiative heat loss, in this example the flame’s circular rim (at its base) has pulled away from the burner due to conductive heat loss. The curvature of the flame’s rim away from, rather than toward, the burner’s edge (which can be faintly seen on the left) is evidence of the flame-burner separation and the approach of the subsequent flame instability.

##### **Week 8: December 4 - 10** 

On GMT 339, 18 flames were ignited for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment. In this experiment, the University of Maryland’s Dept. of Fire Protection Engineering studies microgravity flammability in quiescent (i.e., no-breeze) atmospheres. These tests were conducted using methane, which is the primary component of natural gas, as the fuel. By innovatively matching key properties, BRE uses gaseous fuels to study the flammability of solid and liquid materials. These tests were conducted in oxygen concentrations of up to 40% with a goal of investigating fire risks in atmospheres being considered for future spacecraft and habitats.

150 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **ACME BRE Test Point Image** 



This preliminary false-colored image of a dome-shaped flame shows an array of 14-micron ceramic fibers making temperature measurements. The four visible fibers pass through or near the flame and glow because of the high temperature. This flame, which is non-premixed, is like a bubble in that the methane fuel is inside the bubble and the oxygen is outside the bubble. The flame shell, like the film of a bubble, is a thin sheet where the methane and oxygen come together and react, producing carbon dioxide, water vapor, light, and heat. The fibers are only visible where they are hot, near the flame shell, which is why the centers of the three lower fibers are not visible. 

The temperature profile along each fiber is optically determined from its varying hue through a technique known as Thin Filament Pyrometry (TFP). In this image, the blue has been enhanced so that the soot-free flame is clearly visible along with the much brighter fibers, which naturally transition from red to orange to yellow as the temperature increases. 

##### **Week 9: December 11 - 17** 

On GMT 346, 12 flames were ignited for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment. To investigate the microgravity flammability of solid materials, these tests burned nitrogen-diluted ethylene fuel (50% ethylene/50% nitrogen). Generally, the flames continued to burn through their test duration, where some exhibited partial detachment from the burner’s rim or partial quenching—creating a hole in the flame top. Other flames were quasi-steady, which means changes were occurring at such a slow rate that the flame could be considered constant. The results from these tests may guide material selection for future spacecraft. 

On GMT 350, Sergey Kud-Sverchkov replaced the gas bottles at CIR Manifold #2 and CIR Manifold #4 to support upcoming ACME BRE test points using pure ethylene fuel.

temperature measurements from the color of the glowing fibers. In the second half of the test, the flame begins to locally quench due to radiative heat loss. The hole allows the fuel and oxygen, which are normally separated by the flame sheet, to mix. This mixing enhances flammability and causes the flame to reestablish and the hole to close. ##### **Week 10: December 18 - 24** 

On GMT 352, ten nitrogen-diluted ethylene flames were ignited for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment. Ethylene’s (C2H4) C=C double bond can create sooty flames. These tests were conducted in oxygen concentrations ranging between 32% to 40%. BRE’s goal is to improve our understanding of material flammability in microgravity and thereby guide the selection of materials used in spacecraft.

###### **CAPTION TO VIDEO,** **_WHICH IS NOT INCLUDED IN THIS LOG_** 

Upon ignition, the ethylene flame in this video clip exhibits a lot of soot, which is bright whitishyellow in appearance. On Earth, soot is normally consumed as it passes through the flame sheet, but in this test point the soot particles are large enough to escape the flame. In microgravity, soot growth can be enhanced because it stays within the flame longer due to the absence of buoyancy. Despite this enhanced residence time, the soot fades away quickly as the burn continues. High temperatures are required to create the soot and microgravity conditions can lead to lower flame temperatures. There is no buoyancy to bring in fresh oxygen and the combustion products blanket these low-momentum flames. The carbon dioxide, water vapor, and soot contribute to radiative heat loss. This video clip ends as the flame becomes mostly blue and soot-free. 

##### **Week 11: December 25 - 31** 

On GMT 358, 17 ethylene flames were ignited for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment. These tests were conducted in oxygen concentrations ranging from 24% to 40%, and the ethylene fuel was generally diluted with nitrogen. At the higher oxygen concentrations, the flames were generally quasisteady and continued to burn until the fuel was shut off. In contrast, at the lower oxygen concentrations, the flames all self-extinguished. On GMT 364, Sergey Kud-Sverchkov replaced the gas bottle at CIR Manifold #2 to support continuation of ACME BRE test points. 

152 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

###### **ACME BRE Graph** 



This graph shows as a function of time, the fuel flow (red) and three optical flame measurements from a test in which pure ethylene was burned. The optical flame measurements were made with Photomultiplier Tube (PMT) modules of OH* chemiluminescence (purple), CH* chemiluminescence (light blue), and broadband emissions in the visible and near UV spectrum (dark blue). OH* and CH* are transient molecules which, in the process of chemiluminescence, emit a photon at specific wavelengths when they drop to a lower energy level. The CH* chemiluminescence is the principal source of the blue seen in hydrocarbon flames, while OH* is in the ultraviolet and is used in ACME to indicate ignition and extinction. 

The initially sooty flame was ignited at 32 seconds, after which the hot-wire igniter was retracted. At about 60 seconds, after the ceramic fiber array was inserted, the flame became quasi-steady. But the PMT signals weakened with time, where the low-momentum flame became increasingly blanketed by carbon dioxide and water vapor in the absence of buoyancy. 

At about 95 seconds, the increasing radiative loss caused the flame’s top to quench, creating a hole in the flame. This hole allowed the fuel and oxygen, which are normally separated by the flame, to directly mix. This mixing enhanced flammability, and the flame quickly reestablished and closed the hole, but then again, it locally quenched. This oscillatory behavior happened repeatedly at roughly 1 Hz with the flame opening growing with time until the flame became just a ring. We saw this phenomenon in the first round of BRE testing performed on ISS in 2019. A paper about computational modeling of it is now in press. 

At about 135 seconds, the flame began to partially detach from the rim of the burner and then reattach until it completely extinguished at 148 seconds.

### **<mark>2021</mark>** 

##### **Week 14: January 15 - 21** 

In support of ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points, Victor Glover removed the broken fiber and replaced the igniter tip on the ACME Chamber Insert on GMT 018. On GMT 020, 15 ethylene flames were ignited for the ACME BRE experiment. The array of ceramic fibers (now with three instead of five functional fibers) was successfully used to determine the temperature distributions where the fibers were hot enough to visibly glow. Tests were conducted in atmospheres being considered for future spacecraft and habitats, where that included elevated oxygen concentrations and reduced pressures which can facilitate EVA preparation. At normal atmospheric conditions (e.g., ~21% oxygen by volume), the flames all self-extinguished. In contrast, three of the five tests conducted at 30-35% oxygen and 0.57 bar (i.e., 8.2 psia) featured quasi-steady flames, which continued to burn through the test duration. While the oxygen concentration dominates the flame behavior, this day’s tests will enable a better understanding of the role of the atmospheric pressure on microgravity flammability. On GMT 021, Kate Rubins replaced the gas bottles at CIR Manifold #2 and CIR Manifold #4 to support continuation of ACME BRE test points. 

##### **Week 15: January 22 - 28** 

In support of ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) test points, Kate Rubins replaced the burner in the ACME Chamber Insert on GMT 025. In contrast to the 25-mm burner that was removed, this burner is 50 mm in diameter and allows for larger flames. Tests are being conducted with this larger burner to investigate the dependence of the microgravity flammability on flame size. 

##### **Week 16: January 29 – February 4** 

On GMT 028, the final test points were performed for the ACME BRE (Burning Rate Emulator) experiment led by researchers from the University of Maryland and Russia’s Peter the Great St. Petersburg Polytechnic University. In the study of microgravity flammability, 13 flames were ignited with the 50-mm diameter burner at oxygen concentrations from 40% to 23% in 1 atm. This larger burner allows for larger flames compared to the 25-mm burner that was used for previous tests. Generally, these larger flames tended to self-extinguish due to radiative heat loss, which scales with the flame size. In contrast, the smaller flames on the 25-mm burner were generally quasi-steady. BRE studies material flammability with the goal of improving spacecraft fire safety. 

On GMT 034, Kate Rubins and Victor Glover configured the ACME Chamber Insert to support the next ACME experiment, CLD Flame Part 3 (Coflow Laminar Diffusion). The general goal of CLD Flame is to extend the range of flame conditions that can be accurately predicted, especially under conditions where the flame is sooty or could become unstable. The results could lead to the improved design of practical combustion devices on Earth. 

###### **CAPTION TO VIDEO,** **_WHICH IS NOT INCLUDED IN THIS LOG_** 

This clip from a test point video shows a flame’s oscillatory “death throes” leading ultimately to the flame’s extinction (not shown in the clip). Initially, radiative heat loss causes the top of the flame (on the video’s left) to locally quench, creating a hole in the flame. That opening allows the fuel (from the burner) and oxygen (in the chamber) to directly mix, which increases the flammability and causes the flame to reestablish. But, due to heat loss, the flame partially quenches again, and the opening grows with each oscillation cycle. Later, the flame also begins to detach from the burner’s rim, leaving an opening at the flame’s base (on the video’s right). Here too the flame reestablishes but then opens again—the opening growing until it extends from the 

154 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022 

burner to the flame’s top. At this point in the flame’s powerful death throes, it begins swirling around the burner’s rim. This behavior is notably evident in the glowing ceramic fibers, which can be seen to glow on alternating ends when heated by the swirling flame. To gain a better understanding of this extinction phenomena, the local temperatures of the flame will be derived from the glowing fibers.

- <mark>BRE focuses on fire prevention in spacecraft, examining burning conditions and the flammability of materials in microgravity. ##### **<u><mark>2020 September 14: GMT 258</mark></u>** 

**<mark>Burning Rate Emulator (BRE):</mark>** <mark>The crew reconfigured the ACME hardware in preparation for BRE experiment runs. This includes swapping in Igniter Arm #2. The Burning Rate Emulator (BRE) investigation is conducted in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) on the International Space Station (ISS), as part of the Advanced Combustion via Microgravity Experiments (ACME) project. In this fire safety study, the flammability of solid and liquid materials is simulated by burning gaseous fuels under key conditions corresponding to the solid and liquid materials. This technique has been demonstrated for a wide variety of materials in normal-gravity and could provide an efficient way to screen and select fire-resistant materials for use in spacecraft, if the technique is similarly effective in microgravity.</mark>

CIR powered ops for today have been cancelled while the ground team discusses the issue.  This was performed in support of the continuing BRE part 2 (Burning Rate Emulator) investigation, which is one of the ACME suite of five gas combustion experiments.  The BRE experiment runs are focused on spacecraft fire prevention. More specifically, BRE’s objective is to improve our fundamental understanding of materials flammability and to assess the relevance of existing flammability test methods for low and partial-gravity environments.</mark>

#### **Appendix N. Summary of the ISS data collection** 

The BRE data is all digital and includes both image and non-image data, where this is true for the other ACME experiments.  To be clear, there was no collection of soot or other physical samples that were subsequently transported to the ground. 

The BRE image data includes images from the following cameras: 

1. ACME DataCam, used with a clear fused-silica ‘filter’ and various zoom and focus settings (but those settings were consistent within each individual test) 

2. CFI intensified camera, typically used with a CH* filter (and likely always or nearly always for BRE) 

3. High-Bitdepth Multispectral (HiBMs) camera, equipped with a red filter corresponding to the opposing CIR illumination package, which was sometimes left off 

The raw DataCam images are in a Halcon Iconic Object (hobj) format, while the images from the CFI, and HiBMs cameras are provided in a Tagged Image File (tif) format.  In contrast, the analog video was recorded on the ground in a Transport Stream (ts) format. 

The key non-imaging data from a test is a comma-delimited file that is described in detail in the Software Interface Design Description (ACME-DOC-010 Rev. D). 

The raw data is currently archived at NASA and work is underway to make it available publicly through NASA’s Physical Science Informatics System (PSI). 

174 

ACME/BRE Final Report (2022) 

1 Feb. 2022
